#5045. The 8 Best Beard Oils for Men in 2026, Tested by Grooming Experts

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

The right oil fixes everything, like itch, frizz, and dryness. These are the ones worth your time.

THERE ARE PLENTY of reasons to grow a beard—extra protection from the sun and cold, having something to caress when thinking, or, in my case, faking a sharper jawline. But for most guys, the biggest motivator is convenience. A beard may be easier than daily shaving, but it still needs attention. You have to learn how to trim it, shape it, and use the right beard products to keep it looking good. And no product pulls more weight than a quality beard oil.

Facial hair is naturally coarser and drier, and without proper care, it can feel itchy, scraggly, and uncomfortable. That’s where beard oil comes in. It acts like a conditioner tailored for your face, softening the hair while keeping the skin underneath hydrated and healthy. Dudes with big, full beards may struggle to work a thick balm through all that hair, making a lightweight oil the easier, more effective option.

But beard oils aren’t just for lumberjack-level growth. They help moisturize, reduce itch, and protect any style, from stubble to a full mane. The short version: When your skin and hair stay hydrated, everything looks and feels better.

This beard oil offers the most wide-ranging benefits of any I’ve tested. It hydrates, softens, soothes, exfoliates, and even keeps my beard smelling clean, which is exactly why we gave it a Men’s Health Smell Fresh Award last year. The lightweight blend of argan, rice bran, and apple seed oils smooths out scruff while keeping the skin underneath hydrated, and the papaya and lavender oils gently buff away flakes and calm irritation without leaving any greasy shine behind.

The rosemary-powered deodorizing complex is also a game-changer, neutralizing odor so my mustache and beard stay fresh from morning to night. And because it works on everything—from stubble to a full, dense beard—it’s one of the most versatile grooming products I’ve added to my routine. Clean, subtle in scent, and ridiculously effective, it’s the definition of a multitasker. The beard oil version of a walking desk.

Read More: 2026 Men's Health Smell Fresh Awards

What grabbed me right away about this beard oil is how much hydration you get from such a clean, simple formula. It’s packed with the heavy hitters—jojoba, argan, avocado, grapeseed—basically a greatest-hits lineup for soft, healthy whiskers. Even though it’s marketed toward guys with thick, rough beards, I found it surprisingly lightweight and easy to use on shorter growth, too.

Because the oil absorbs so well, I only need a drop or two to keep longer or bushier beards smooth for hours. And if you’re prone to breakouts, relax: the formula is non-comedogenic, so it won’t clog your pores or leave your skin angry. It’s also a no-brainer for anyone who avoids fragrance.

While this stuff isn’t completely unscented, the fragrance is as minimal as it gets. I have sensitive skin and saw no irritation, as well as no cologne cloud living under my nose all day. If you prefer your beard oil to work without announcing itself, this one’s ideal.

This beard oil has the richest texture of all I’ve tried, thanks to its avocado-oil base. Long beards burn through moisture quickly, and this formula actually keeps up, sinking in deep to soften and smooth without leaving a heavy, greasy layer behind. Squalane—one of my favorite skincare ingredients—lightens the blend just enough so my beard feels conditioned and controlled instead of weighed down.

You also get the skin benefits of vitamin E, which helps calm irritation under the beard and keeps everything feeling comfortable throughout the day. The scent is low-key but luxe: a mix of cedarwood, jasmine, and sandalwood that feels warm and spa-like before disappearing into the background. And despite the small bottle, a few drops are all you need, even if your beard’s edging into Gandalf territory.

I know I said never to buy a beard oil for the scent, but this option is an exception. With a rich palo santo fragrance that also blends in bright cardamom and dry papyrus, this beard oil makes my beard smell clean, smooth, and surprisingly refined. It keeps that freshness going all day, no matter how long my beard gets.

The formula does serious work too: on shorter growth, it calms irritation and helps keep ingrowns from flaring up, and on longer beards, it replaces the moisture facial hair naturally loses, softening dry ends and hydrating the skin underneath. A few drops give me a conditioned, healthy-looking beard that smells incredible without ever overpowering my face.

Hair oil is the most underrated styling product for men, as it hydrates and shields strands to create a smoother base that helps other products apply better and work more effectively. I’ve also found that it’s the easiest to forget. When I’ve recently put my brain cells through the wringer and face a subsequent rise in forgetfulness, a dual-purpose beard and hair oil is my savior—and none are better than Aesop’s

No matter how out of it I may be, I always remember to apply my beard oil (my face is way too itchy without it), so the bottle serves as a nice reminder to run my hands through my head as well. Barely half a vial full is enough to cover both my beard and hair, leaving both feeling softer and more hydrated. Plus, this is Aesop, so the scent is incredible. It has an earthy, bright fragrance that continues Aesop’s trend of being the only brand to pull off essential oil/incense-type scents.

I have a slew of skin and scalp issues, but one area in grooming I’ve never struggled with is growing hair (he said, knocking profusely on wood). Still, when I’m in the early stages of growing a beard, it’s nice to have a boost. That’s where Asna’s aptly named Beard Boost Oil comes in. It’s loaded with vitamins A, C, E, and B to support healthier and stronger strands, while squalane deeply hydrates and smooths the hair shaft. Together, they reduce dryness and frizz so your beard appears denser, softer, and fuller.

To be clear, it won’t necessarily help your beard grow, but it will help it appear thicker, which is the next best thing. The sandalwood scent is subtle and pleasant, so I never have to worry about it clashing with stronger fragrances. This oil is also the thickest on this list, which takes some getting used to. However, once you do, it’s a positive because you only need to use a couple of drops to cover your beard.

All but one beard oil on this list comes in one-ounce bottles (Aesop is 0.8 ounces), which makes it even more impressive that Horace’s lasts noticeably longer. The dropper dispenses tiny amounts at a time, preventing overuse, and the lightweight formula spreads easily—I only need two to three drops, depending on my beard length.

It’s also the best pick for guys who prefer a fresher scent. While most beard oils lean woody or earthy, Horace stands out with a subtle lemon-and-mint blend that won’t clash with amber or wood-forward colognes but still feels like a clean break from the usual.

If you’re anything like me, you drink roughly two pots of coffee a day. Just the smell of it gives me a burst of energy, which is what I love about this beard oil’s caffeinated scent. After a rough night or ahead of a busy day when I’ll need as much energy as I can get, getting whiffs of this beard oil gives me an added boost—or at least helps remind me to have another cup.

I also have a love of brown liquor that was likely preordained by my Irish heritage, so the hint of whisky scent in this oil is welcomed, though the smell of coffee is stronger. This is probably a good thing; I don’t need a reminder to have another one of those. Performance-wise, Viking Revolution is as effective as you can find under $10.

Its 100 percent argan and jojoba oils blend keeps whiskers feeling soft for hours. That being said, I do find I need to use a little bit more of this stuff to cover my entire beard than other options on this list. But for the price point and scent, it’s well worth it.

What really makes or breaks a beard oil is the blend of oils inside. You want ingredients that actually penetrate the hair and nourish the skin—not heavy oils that sit on top and leave your beard feeling slick and suffocated. Look for lightweight, fast-absorbing oils like jojoba, argan, grapeseed, avocado, and castor, which sink in quickly without leaving a greasy film. These not only soften your whiskers but also keep the skin beneath your beard healthy and hydrated.

Because beard oil is massaged from the roots to the ends, anything too thick can clog pores underneath, leading to irritation or those deep, painful beard pimples every guy dreads. Smart formulas use blends of light oils—often combined with extras like marula, sweet almond, squalane, or vitamin E—to boost moisture, strengthen hair, and calm inflamed skin without weighing things down. As long as the base oils are lightweight and absorbent, a multi-oil mix is exactly what you want.

Fragrance shouldn’t be the main reason you reach for a beard oil, especially if you have sensitive skin, but it can be a great bonus. Plenty of beard oils have masculine, cologne-quality scents that give the product some added value. Plus, it’ll give you something pleasant to smell on your mustache all day.

That being said, strong scents can easily trigger irritation, so stick to oils with a subtle aroma that comes naturally from the ingredients themselves. Added fragrances are common irritants, and even high concentrations of essential oils can cause redness or flare-ups.

If you’re not sure how your skin will react, do a quick patch test. Apply a small amount to one spot, then wait a few hours (or even overnight). No redness, itching, or bumps? You’re in the clear and good to use it everywhere.

I’ve had a beard for so long that I barely remember my face without one, which means I’ve spent the better part of two decades testing every type of beard product out there, including more oils than I can count. Over the past two years, I’ve compared notes with fellow Men’s Health editors, swapped favorites, and put a fresh batch of formulas through daily testing to see which ones actually deliver softer, healthier, better-looking whiskers.

With all that experience, I was able to narrow the field before spending months trying different oils on everything from short stubble to full-grown scruff. I looked at how well they hydrated, how fast they absorbed, how they felt on the skin underneath, and whether they left any greasy residue behind. Price, scent, and ingredient quality all factored in, too. What you’ll find below are my honest picks for the best beard oils worth buying in 2026, options that truly elevate your grooming routine, whether you’re rocking light stubble or a full-on mane.

For the best results, I like to treat my beard the same way I treat the hair on my head: regular washing and consistent conditioning. Clean it a few times a week with a beard-specific wash to remove buildup, then follow with a good beard oil. Before applying the oil, smooth out your beard with a comb, brush, or even your fingers so you’re starting with the shape you want.

Warm a small amount of oil between your palms and work in from the cheeks and chin, massaging it in the same way you’d apply a face moisturizer. Make sure you’re getting all the way down to the skin—that’s what keeps dryness, itch, and irritation away. If your beard is longer, finish by running your fingers through the length in downward strokes to coat every strand.

#5043. Want to Lower Your Dementia Risk? This Simple, Free Habit May Help

It’s good for other areas of health, too.

Getting plenty of sunlight each day is linked to several health benefits, including a lowered risk of depression and better sleep. And now new research suggests another perk to add to the list: a reduced risk of dementia.

That's the major takeaway from a new General Psychiatry study where researchers found a link between more daytime light exposure and a significantly lower risk of dementia in study participants. Sunlight exposure isn't currently considered a big factor in dementia prevention, but the findings make a strong case for it. Here’s what neurologists say about this news.

Meet the experts: Anna Hohler, MD, director of neurology for Northwell Health's Westchester Region, and chair of neurology at Phelps Hospital and Northern Westchester Hospital, and Clifford Segil, DO, a neurologist at Providence Saint John's Health Center

For the observational study, researchers analyzed data from 87,577 adults who wore wrist devices with accelerometers for seven days that also measured exposure to daytime light. The team then followed up with the participants after about 8.1 years. During that time, 741 of them were diagnosed with dementia.

They discovered that participants who were exposed to average daytime light levels higher than 1,000 lux (similar to an overcast day) had a 16 percent lower relative risk of developing dementia. Those who spent longer periods of time in brighter daytime light (at least 5,000 lux) had a 17 percent lower relative risk of dementia.

The amount of outdoor light exposure mattered, too. In one model, people who got less than 42 minutes of bright daytime light exposure each day were more likely to develop dementia than those who had six established risk factors for dementia (including alcohol use, obesity, air pollution, and hearing loss).

"Further research should explore its role in dementia screening and inform the development of light-based interventions," the researchers wrote.

It's crucial to stress that the study found a link between daylight exposure and lowered dementia risk, but it didn't prove cause and effect. It also just uncovered an association—researchers didn't explore what may be behind it. Still, experts have a few theories.

"Regular exposure to daylight may support several factors that are important for brain health," says Anna Hohler, MD, director of neurology for Northwell Health's Westchester Region, and chair of neurology at Phelps Hospital and Northern Westchester Hospital. People who spend more time outdoors tend to be more physically active, socially engaged, and have healthier sleep-wake cycles, all of which have been linked to a lower risk of cognitive decline, Dr. Hohler says. Therefore, the amount of daylight exposure measured may be “a marker of a broader pattern of healthy behaviors,” she says.

Sunlight itself could be a factor, since Dr. Hohler says it helps regulate the body's circadian rhythm. This influences sleep quality, hormone regulation, metabolism, and brain function. "Good sleep is essential for memory consolidation and the clearance of waste products from the brain," she points out. Your body also produces vitamin D when exposed to the sun, which may also be linked to these benefits, “although it is likely only one piece of a much larger picture," Dr. Hohler says.

Finally, people who spend time outdoors tend to be more physically and socially active, habits which are strongly associated with maintaining cognitive health, according to Clifford Segil, DO, neurologist at Providence Saint John's Health Center. “To obtain the increased sunlight exposure does require you to be out and about, which activates the social pathways in your brain,” he says. These behaviors also have far-reaching benefits beyond dementia risk. “Maintaining social connections throughout your life has been shown to be brain healthy and just good for you.”

Again, the study didn't prove that spending more time outside will magically prevent you from developing dementia. But it certainly makes a case for including time outside as part of your daily routine. But what should you aim for?

"A practical goal is to spend some time outdoors every day, particularly in the morning when natural light helps reinforce healthy circadian rhythms," Dr. Hohler says. She suggests scheduling a daily walk, exercising outdoors, gardening, or even sipping your coffee outside. These habits "can increase light exposure, while also encouraging physical activity and social interaction," Dr. Hohler says.

You can also get your friends involved by scheduling walks together to catch up instead of sitting inside and sipping a glass of wine, per Dr. Segil. “Choose an outdoor social activity...to maximize the benefits of possible sunlight, movement, and friendship, which may all be brain healthy,” he says.

But, of course, don't neglect other habits to support your brain health. "Sunlight should be viewed as one component of a brain-healthy lifestyle that also includes regular exercise, adequate sleep, a nutritious diet, and social engagement," Dr. Hohler says.

Carina Hsieh, MPH, is the deputy features editor of Women’s Health. She has more than a decade’s worth of experience working in media and has covered everything from beauty, fashion, travel, lifestyle, pets, to health.

She began her career as an intern in the fashion closet at Cosmopolitan where she worked her way up to Senior Sex & Relationships Editor. While covering women’s health there, she discovered her passion for health service journalism and took a break to get her Masters in Public Health. Post-grad school, she worked as a freelance writer and as The Daily Beast’s first Beauty, Health, and Wellness Reporter.

Carina is an alum of the Fashion Institute of Technology and the Yale School of Public Health. She and her French Bulldog, Bao Bao, split their time between Brooklyn and Connecticut. She enjoys reformer Pilates, (slow) running, and smelling the fancy toiletries in boutique fitness class locker rooms.

#5042. Zcash's NU7 Upgrade Goes Live, Recycling 60% of Fees to Fund Future Block Rewards

Zcash's NU7 Upgrade Goes Live, Recycling 60% of Fees to Fund Future Block Rewards

"NU7 has successfully activated on mainnet. A new era of Zcash is here," the project's official account tweeted.

NU7 has successfully activated on mainnet. A new era of Zcash is here. $ZEC pic.twitter.com/rPaSFe9h7j

"This is a huge milestone for Shielded Labs," Jason McGee of Shielded Labs, the Zcash development organization behind the mechanism, tweeted ahead of the activation.

The NSM tackles a problem Zcash inherited from Bitcoin: whether, as block subsidies decline with each halving, transaction fees alone will be enough to pay miners to secure the network. Under the mechanism, 60% of transaction fees will be removed from circulation and gradually reissued through future block rewards, while preserving the 21 million ZEC supply cap. Reissuance will begin in 2031, with the recycled funds spread along a smooth curve on top of the existing halving schedule.

The NSM was Shielded Labs' first project and builds on an idea that developer Nate Wilcox proposed years ago, originally called the Zcash Posterity Fund, McGee said. A separate proposal under consideration would require Zcash Community Grants to contribute 50,000 ZEC from its treasury to the NSM.

— Jason McGee 🛡 (@aquietinvestor) November 5, 2026

NU7 also limits how much private transaction activity can fit into each block, reducing the data wallets must scan to find their payments, according to the upgrade specification.

Shielded Labs' original design would have replaced Zcash's halvings with a smooth issuance curve. Community panels supported that change, but coinholders did not, so an alternative design that kept the halvings was developed instead.

"We still think the original design was simpler and better, but we respect the process that led us here," McGee wrote. "It should be difficult to change the protocol, and the process should resist capture by any single organization, group, or influential individual."

The upgrade arrives as Zcash's community debates the future of Zcash Community Grants (ZCG) and its share of the Dev Fund, the slice of block rewards that pays for development.

The five organizations that coordinate Zcash's coinholder voting, Project Tachyon, Shielded Labs, Valar Group, the Zcash Foundation and ZODL, have set out an expedited timeline for proposals and polling, saying "tensions were high" and that a drawn-out debate risked "deepening divisions within the Zcash community."

Proposals are due by November 8, with polls for coinholders and community panels running from November 16 to November 30. If rough consensus emerges, the groups will support a further network upgrade, NU 7.1, to implement it.

#5040. Zcash NU7 upgrade goes live, leaves 12,224 ZEC stranded in Sprout pool

Zcash’s NU7 upgrade has rendered about $15 million in legacy Sprout funds unspendable, although a future network upgrade could potentially restore access.

Zcash’s NU7 network upgrade activated Friday, making about $15 millionin ZEC held in the network’s legacy Sprout shielded pool unspendable.

The upgrade took effect at block 3,543,405 at 02:34 UTC, according to blockchain tracker ZecStats, which lists the Sprout pool balance at about 12,224 ZEC.

The upgrade cuts Zcash’s target time between blocks from 75 seconds to 25, although actual block intervals will adjust toward the new target as mining difficulty changes.

Under Zcash Improvement Proposal (ZIP) 2003, NU7 rejects version 4 transactions, the only format that supports spending Sprout funds. The remaining coins cannot be transferred under the new rules, but have not been burned.

The specification leaves open the possibility that a future network upgrade could restore access, either by re-enabling version 4 transactions or introducing another mechanism to retrieve the funds. However, it warns that “the ability to spend Sprout funds may never be re-enabled.”

NU7 also introduces a Network Sustainability Mechanism that removes 60% of transaction fees from circulation for eventual reissuance through future mining rewards, starting around February 2031.

The upgrade preserves Zcash’s 21 million ZEC supply cap and halving schedule, while introducing limits on shielded activity designed to reduce wallet synchronization demands.

#5036. Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.5M in Crypto From 35 Investigations

Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.5M in Crypto From 35 Investigations

A former FBI agent has pleaded guilty to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency using wallet credentials the bureau had collected during its own investigations, the Justice Department said on Thursday.

Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, pleaded guilty to wire fraud and money laundering in federal court in Alexandria. He faces up to 20 years in prison and is scheduled to be sentenced on March 4, 2027.

Former FBI agent Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, pled guilty today to wire fraud and money laundering related to using his access to steal cryptocurrency.@FBIWFO https://t.co/jDVtSiAM3H

— U.S. Attorney EDVA (@EDVAnews) November 5, 2026

Yaroch worked at the FBI's Boston Division from 2017 to early 2025 on a squad investigating an unnamed "adversarial nation state," before moving to the bureau's Counterintelligence and Espionage Division, where he had access to FBI systems holding crypto credentials gathered in investigations, according to prosecutors.

Beginning no later than December 2024, he used that access to steal crypto from wallets controlled by subjects of other investigations, by nation-state actors, and from at least one wallet the FBI had created for an operation. He made about 100 fraudulent transfers using credentials from 35 separate FBI investigations, then swapped the stolen funds into other cryptocurrencies and moved them through further transactions to hide their origin. He used some of the money to pay off credit cards, car payments and other loans.

Yaroch also planned a new life abroad. He asked ChatGPT how to maximize profits from investments and where to live in the European Union, and after the chatbot suggested buying a vineyard in Portugal, he gave Portuguese lawyers power of attorney to obtain a tax identification number for him, prosecutors said. He arranged to travel to Portugal in September.

His actions compromised multiple FBI investigations into criminal groups and nation-state adversaries, as well as sources and methods used in foreign intelligence collection, according to the plea agreement's statement of facts.

Yaroch turned himself in. On July 29, he broke down while telling a former colleague at the Justice Department's National Security Division that he had made "very poor decisions" involving crypto wallets, according to an FBI affidavit filed in the case. He then self-reported to FBI headquarters, saying he had become frustrated that the bureau "could not or would not act" against an adversary's crypto accounts and had decided to "take matters into his own hands."

At the time, Yaroch said he had made about 10 to 12 transfers worth under $1 million, far less than the figures in his plea. Agents searching his home two days later seized a Trezor hardware wallet, three passports and a slip of paper bearing seed phrases, according to the affidavit. His Kraken account held about $188,600, mostly in U.S. dollars and the USDC stablecoin, with smaller amounts in Squid (QUID) and Bitcoin. A further $933,800 sat in Suilend, a lending protocol on the Sui blockchain, which he had accessed through the Slush wallet app. Yaroch told agents he chose Suilend "simply because he liked that the logo was a water droplet."

Agents transferred about $925,000 from those accounts to government-controlled wallets, the affidavit said. The roughly $165,600 held as U.S. dollars on Kraken could not be moved the same way.

Decrypt reported on the charges against Yaroch in August.

#5034. How Big of a Calorie Deficit Do You Need to Achieve Body Recomp?

Your calorie deficit could be working against you. Here’s how to find the right balance.

With body recomposition come two goals: lose body fat while gaining muscle. At first glance, it might seem a bit too good to be true, if you think about how each goal is achieved individually. But it’s not.

To lose body fat, you need to eat fewer calories than you burn in a day. To gain muscle, you need to continuously challenge your muscles in the gym, and eat enough to hit your protein goals, which is vital to building muscle mass—especially in a calorie deficit.

With the two goals seemingly at nutritional odds, it’s difficult to determine exactly how you should eat to hit this two-for-one goal. Exactly how big of a calorie deficit do you need to achieve body recomp? We tapped an expert to find out.

Get your personalized plan with our Eating for Body Recomp guide

Achieving body recomp takes some strategic nutritional planning, starting with a balanced calorie goal.

Keep your calorie deficit on the more conservative side when it comes to body recomp so you have enough energy to power your important workouts, Dana White, RD, previously told WH. Aim for a calorie deficit no larger than 250 to 500 calories per day. Any larger than that, and you run the risk of feeling under-fueled in the gym or not hitting your protein goals—which is major for building and maintaining muscle mass.

With body recomp, though, it’s important to get even more granular and understand what’s making up those cals, says White. “It’s not just about eating calories—it’s the type of calories.”

One of the best ways to determine how to fill those calories is by tracking your macronutrients—carbohydrates, proteins, and fats. To find out how much of each macro you should get, check out our Eating for Body Recomp guide.

She’s reported on topics regarding health, nutrition, mental health, fitness, sex, and relationships for several years. #5032. Binance’s $6.3B hack leaves questions over hot wallet safeguards

Experts say blockchain records show repeated wallet drains but cannot establish Binance’s internal signing controls or transaction limits.

Binance’s $6.3 billion security incident has left unanswered questions about how the exchange managed its hot wallets and limited the amount attackers could access.

Binance co-CEO Richard Teng said on Oct. 30 that the attack affected the exchange’s own hot wallet. Most customer assets were held in offline cold storage and moved to hot wallets only when users requested withdrawals.

Wallet balances offer only a partial picture of an exchange’s exposure to an attack. Public blockchain records can show funds moving but cannot establish the internal controls governing those transfers, CertiK analyst Jonathan Riss told Cointelegraph.

In its initial report Tuesday, Binance said attackers accessed an internal wallet-signing service to move funds outside its normal withdrawal process. Binance said some technical details would remain confidential to prevent further attacks.

The exchange did not respond to Cointelegraph’s questions about its wallet balances and safeguards.

Rivals including Coinbase, Gate and Upbit have disclosed their cold-storage ratios following the attack.

Riss said the $6.3 billion figure represented the cumulative value of assets affected across 10 blockchains. On Ethereum, attackers repeatedly drained a hot wallet as new deposits arrived.

When attackers can obtain valid signatures, their potential access depends on what the signing system allows them to transfer over time, he said. A wallet’s balance at a single moment does not capture that exposure.

Related: EIP-7702 didn’t cause Binance hack, but may have amplified losses

Transaction limits enforced by the application requesting a signature can be bypassed if that application is compromised, Riss said. Limits enforced independently by the signing system may still restrict transfers.

“A comparison with other exchanges isn’t really possible from the outside,” Riss added. “Public information on internal signing architecture is limited everywhere, and reserve distribution and proof-of-reserves attestations show what sits where, not how a withdrawal is authorized.”

BitGo chief product officer Eugene Hahr also declined to speculate about Binance’s internal safeguards.

“The breakdown of custody arrangements is a question Binance is best placed to answer,” he said.

Idan Ofrat, co-founder and chief product officer at Fireblocks, said exchanges typically keep enough funds in hot wallets to cover a few days of expected withdrawals, often representing a low single-digit percentage of assets under management.

Exchanges generally hold the bulk of customer assets in cold storage, while some use intermediate wallets that require manual approval for transfers to hot wallets, he said.

Related: Binance traces $6.3B hack to fake job interviews, compromised AI tool

Alex Tobol, chief technology officer at Telegram-based crypto wallet Walt, said holding billions of dollars in hot wallets is not inherently unusual for an exchange of Binance’s size.

Hot wallets generally process withdrawals automatically, Tobol said, but replenishing them from cold storage should require a separate treasury decision and manual approval.

Hahr said controls applied before signing, including transaction limits, approved withdrawal addresses and additional authorization requirements, can help contain losses when a system is compromised.

Magazine: Binance hackers raced to move billions before it could be frozen

#5030. Morning Minute: Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

Morning Minute: Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

📉 Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

On Wednesday, the market decided a Democratic Congress was good news. Stocks had their best day in three months, Bitcoin climbed back over $80,800, and Trump posted on Truth Social that the market “just hit another Record High” with 401(k)s “through the roof.”

On Thursday, the market changed its mind.

The S&P 500 fell 4.36%, its worst session since the Liberation Day tariffs in April 2025. The Nasdaq dropped 5.52% and closed in correction, more than 10% below its record from last month. The Dow shed 1,833 points.

The damage concentrated in one place. The iShares Semiconductor ETF fell more than 12%, its worst day since March 2020. Micron lost 17.5%, AMD 17.3%, Marvell 16.4%, Nvidia 9.3%. The Magnificent Seven shed roughly $1 trillion in market value. The VIX touched 28.09 intraday, its highest since March. By the close, the U.S. stock market had lost $3T in value - more than the entire market cap of crypto.

Notably, the thing that spooked investors was the same election result that lifted everything a day earlier. Several states where Democrats won have active local opposition to data center construction, and the market spent Thursday pricing in slower permitting and tighter oversight of the AI buildout. Ipek Ozkardeskaya at Swissquote put it simply: “We suspect there is going to be some scrutiny on this aggressive AI buildout.” Rate expectations moved too, with December hike odds falling to 70.1% from 82.7%.

Crypto did not sit this one out. Bitcoin fell 5.4% to $75,871, below where it traded before the election. Ethereum dropped 7.6% to $2,239, a steeper fall than the Nasdaq. XRP lost 9.8%, and Hyperliquid fell 4.9%. The CMC20 index fell 6.29%. Almost $1.1B in crypto longs were liquidated on the day, before a slight relief rally overnight.

Friday’s October jobs report is next. After a week where the market read the same political outcome two opposite ways inside two sessions, it is fair to wonder how much anyone actually knows right now. We will find out soon…

#5025. Tesla to Wind Down Cybertruck Production as Elon Musk Pivots to Robotaxis and AI

Tesla to Wind Down Cybertruck Production as Elon Musk Pivots to Robotaxis and AI

Tesla will stop making the Cybertruck at its Texas Gigafactory around the middle of next year, CEO Elon Musk said on Friday, as the company shifts its focus to autonomous vehicles.

"Tesla will be winding down production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas around the middle of next year to make room for increased Cybercab production," Musk tweeted.

Tesla will be winding down production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas around the middle of next year to make room for increased Cybercab production

Responding to a user who called the Cybertruck the best vehicle Tesla has ever made, Musk replied: "True, but the Cybertruck fleet will be around for a long time." "Cybertruck was an incredible technology development platform and lives on through Cybercab," Wes Morrill, the truck's lead engineer, wrote in response to Musk's post, thanking the truck's owners and "everyone who contributed to making the future looking like the future."

The stainless-steel pickup, first delivered to customers in late 2023, has struggled to win buyers. Tesla delivered 8,295 vehicles in its "other models" category, which includes the Cybertruck, in the third quarter, down 48% from a year earlier, Electrek reported. That puts the truck's sales at roughly a tenth of the 250,000 units a year that Tesla built its production line to make, according to the outlet.

Musk has made the Cybercab, Tesla's robotaxi, central to his push to turn the carmaker into an AI and robotics company. In April 2025, he said Tesla would launch its first robotaxi service in Austin using Model Y vehicles running its Full Self-Driving software, and begin building Cybercabs in Texas the following year.

Tesla began offering rides in the Cybercab, a two-seater with no steering wheel or pedals that was first unveiled in October 2024, in limited areas of Austin on September 3, Reuters reported. The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration said at the time that it was "evaluating the situation," as federal rules typically require cars to have human controls. Tesla began producing the Cybercab in April, and had 45 of them registered in Texas as of the launch, according to the report.

At the 2024 unveiling, Musk said he expects the Cybercab to cost around $30,000, Auto Express reported.

The shift comes as Musk weighs combining Tesla with SpaceX, which absorbed his AI company xAI earlier this year. Last month, Musk said both companies' boards had formed special committees of independent directors to evaluate a merger, which would reshape his AI empire by bringing rockets, satellites, AI models, robots and cars under one roof. A SpaceX filing has since confirmed that any combination would need sign-off from its "controlling shareholder," Musk.

A merger would also create a Bitcoin treasury behemoth, combining SpaceX's 18,712 BTC and Tesla's 11,509 BTC for a total of 30,221 BTC, worth roughly $2.3 billion at current prices.

Both companies have also blocked their shares from being offered on the tokenized stock venue being built by crypto exchange OKX and NYSE owner ICE.

#5023. GOPAX halts crypto trading, withdrawals to move all assets to cold storage

South Korea’s GOPAX suspended trading, deposits and standard withdrawals after a hacking threat, with manual withdrawals available through support.

South Korean crypto exchange GOPAX suspended trading, deposits and standard withdrawals Friday after receiving an email threatening a hack, according to local newspaper Kyunghyang Shinmun.

A GOPAX representative told the newspaper that no hack or attempted theft had occurred at that point.

GOPAX said it halted deposits and standard withdrawals at 5:55 am UTC. Trading stopped at 6:53 UTC, according to a second announcement. All unfilled orders were canceled.

GOPAX said it plans to transfer all crypto assets to offline wallets to protect users following a series of leaks and hacking incidents affecting financial institutions. Its notices cited financial regulators’ recommendations.

Cointelegraph reached out to GOPAX for comment but had not received a response by publication time.

Customers can still request withdrawals through GOPAX’s support center, but the exchange warned that manual verification and approvals could delay processing.

The suspension came days after South Korea’s Financial Services Commission (FSC) urged domestic exchanges to increase cold-wallet holdings following the $6.3 billion Binance hack. South Korean exchanges must keep at least 80% of customer crypto assets in cold storage.

Financial Supervisory Service data previously showed that GOPAX held 80.54% of user assets offline at the end of September, near the required minimum.

In October, the FSC reported cybersecurity deficiencies at 130 of 555 financial companies reviewed nationwide. The regulator also identified GOPAX operator Streami and crypto exchange Prabang among 16 companies involved in earlier security incidents, prompting plans for tighter cybersecurity oversight.

South Korean exchange Prabang also suspended services after detecting abnormal withdrawals from its hot wallet on Oct. 24. The exchange reported about 100 million won ($70,000) in losses, according to Yonhap News Agency. Prabang said it would reimburse affected customers using company funds.

#5021. $3.1B in Stolen Binance Crypto Has Yet to Be Laundered: Elliptic

$3.1B in Stolen Binance Crypto Has Yet to Be Laundered: Elliptic

Around half of the $6.43 billion stolen from Binance last month has yet to be laundered, blockchain analytics firm Elliptic said in a report updated on Friday.

About $3.11 billion remains in addresses linked to the theft, mostly in ETH and Bitcoin, and those funds are "very likely" still held by North Korea, Elliptic said. A further $2.85 billion, or 44% of the haul, has been frozen or seized, mostly stablecoins that issuers such as Tether and Circle froze within an hour of the theft. That compares with around 3% frozen after the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in February 2025, according to Elliptic.

Binance hack update: the XRP has started moving. About $50 million has been swapped, mostly through THORChain into BTC, with about $226 million still to move.

Bitcoin converted from other stolen assets is now about 1,650 BTC ($124 million), almost twice the figure a day earlier.…

The FBI has attributed the October 29 theft to North Korean hackers it calls TraderTraitor. North Korea has denied involvement.

The laundering so far has been "highly complex and clearly automated," Elliptic said. It has used more than 20,000 wallets on 16 blockchains, at least 36 services and at least 65,000 transactions, roughly one new wallet every 30 seconds.

The 18,270 BTC stolen from Binance, worth about $1.5 billion at the time of the theft, was split into 19 addresses on the day of the hack and has not moved since. The nine largest Ethereum addresses, holding 89% of the attacker's ETH, have not moved since October 30.

Most of the laundering so far, about $220 million in TRX, has run through Tron, where a single distribution wallet sent 684.6 million TRX to hundreds of new addresses over 51 hours, including through the night in Pyongyang. Almost all of it was bridged to Ethereum.

All of the stolen Solana, about $87 million at current prices, has been swapped, bridged or mixed, and the attacker has started laundering the stolen XRP, with about $50 million converted so far and $226 million remaining. In total, about $124 million has been converted into Bitcoin, much of it through cross-chain service THORChain.

The speed has fueled speculation that AI agents are moving the funds, but Elliptic said the evidence points to conventional automation. "The laundering is clearly automated, but nothing we have seen shows an AI agent making decisions," the firm said.

Elliptic expects the attacker to swap the ETH for Bitcoin and then cash out through OTC brokers and exchanges over a period of months. After the Bybit hack, North Korea laundered all 499,000 stolen ETH within 10 days but took months to cash out, the firm noted.

Binance has continued to restore services. On Friday, the exchange said users could now verify that their balances are backed at least 1:1 using the zero-knowledge proof for its latest proof-of-reserves snapshot, and that it aims to reopen withdrawals for all tokens on restored networks by the end of next week.

The valuation now in question is already well below earlier expectations. In September, Decrypt reported that a leaked draft filing showed Anthropic was seeking a valuation of around $2 trillion.

#5018. Greece approves 10% tax on crypto gains starting in 2027

Greece has approved a 10% tax on cryptocurrency gains starting in 2027, with an exemption for investors whose annual net gains do not exceed 500 euros.

Greece’s parliament approved legislation imposing a 10% tax on cryptocurrency gains, establishing new rules for investors as the country prepares to introduce the levy in 2027.

The Hellenic Parliament passed the tax measures Thursday as part of a broader bill covering loan oversight, debt protections and financial market regulation.

The bill introduces a 10% tax on profits from crypto asset sales but exempts investors whose annual gains do not exceed 500 euros ($560), local financial publication Oikonomikos Tachydromos reported Friday. The new framework is scheduled to take effect on Jan. 1, 2027.

The legislation also imposes a 15% tax on income from staking, crypto lending and providing liquidity to decentralized protocols. Using crypto to buy goods or services may also trigger capital gains tax, while swapping one crypto asset for another does not generate taxable gains, the report notes.

Greece’s Ministry of National Economy and Finance first proposed the tax in October. The legislation gives investors 12 months to voluntarily declare previously unreported crypto gains. They must pay the resulting tax within 60 days of filing to avoid penalties and interest.

#5017. Crypto Liquidations Top $1 Billion as Bitcoin Tests 11-Week Low

Crypto Liquidations Top $1 Billion as Bitcoin Tests 11-Week Low

Bitcoin is trading near $75,570 on Friday after touching $74,780.91 today; its lowest intraday reading since August 21, 77 days earlier.

The crypto market retreat followed a brutal selloff in AI stocks and another round of forced closures in derivatives markets.

According to CoinGlass data, $1.05 billion in crypto have been liquidated over the preceding 24 hours. Long positions, which profit when prices rise, accounted for $930.04 million, or 88.2%, compared with $124.69 million in shorts.

That 80%-20% gap in liquidations also matches the overall sentiment tracked by the Fear and Greed Index which is set at 28 points (approaching extreme fear) out of 100, where 0 is a state of maximum fear among traders, and 100 is maximum greed. For context, the index was at a neutral just a week ago, before the midterm elections.

Bitcoin accounted for $267.20 million of the total, with 88.9% from long positions, while Ethereum recorded $167.14 million, 81.1% of it long.

Liquidation happens when an exchange automatically closes a position because its collateral is no longer sufficient, sometimes adding selling pressure to an existing decline. Unlike normal trading, in which investors can handle any unrealized loss, trading with leverage gives a maneuver window that can end in the loss of all funds once an asset goes beyond or below a specific price.

The post-midterm election fear is spreading on Wall Street, and U.S. technology stocks took the first big hit on Thursday. The Nasdaq Composite fell 5.52%, its worst session in 19 months, while the Philadelphia semiconductor index plunged more than 12% and Nvidia alone lost over 9%.

Democratic victories in Tuesday's midterm elections raised concerns that tougher AI regulation could slow construction of the data centers behind this year's chip rally. The S&P 500 lost 4.36% on Thursday, erasing roughly $3 trillion in market value across its constituent companies.

Bitcoin had already given back its post-election relief rally as chipmakers sank, as Decrypt reported Thursday. Forced closures of bullish crypto positions accelerated the fall, though simultaneous moves in stocks and tokens doesn’t necessarily mean the equity rout alone caused Bitcoin's decline.

A stronger-than-expected U.S. October jobs report also increased expectations of another Federal Reserve rate hike in December. Higher borrowing costs can weigh on speculative markets, leaving crypto exposed even as chip shares recover.

Right now, Bitcoin trades near $75,570, with the current candle still forming after a low of $74,780.91. The weekly chart shows another red candle and a sequence of lower highs since September, when Bitcoin briefly traded above $87,000.

The 50-day exponential moving average, or EMA, gives more weight to recent prices and sits near $79,756, above the 200-day EMA around $76,497. That ordering remains bullish on a longer view, but Bitcoin is now trading below both averages, a sign the latest decline has overtaken the lagging bullish signal.

The Relative Strength Index, or RSI, reads 37.7, below the neutral 50 mark but still above the 30 level generally considered oversold. The Average Directional Index, or ADX, is at 19.7, typical of trendless states—but the negative directional indicator exceeds the positive one, pointing to weak but bearish directional pressure.

The immediate support is $74,780, the horizontal line and intraday low on the supplied chart. A daily close below it would shift attention toward the $73,000 to $72,500 area around August's breakout, followed by the psychological $70,000 level if that zone fails.

On a recovery, Bitcoin first faces the 200-day EMA near $76,497, then some weak and immediate near $80,000. Friday's daily candle is still open, with its $74,780.91 low the immediate level to defend.

#5015. 7 Pendant Lights to Perfect Your Overhead Lighting Scheme (2026)

Sure, recessed lighting serves a functional purpose, but pendant lights are where things get interesting. A quick sweep through any home tour, and you’ll recognize the strategically placed Louis Poulsen above the kitchen island, an Isamu Noguchi in the entryway, and a Herman Miller saucer pendant hovering over the living room that will steal the show. Below are seven pendant light fixtures that set the mood instead of harshening the vibe. We rounded up the best pendant lights for various design personalities, including a vintage-inspired showpiece and a few variations of low-key ambient lighting. Among them are most of the different types of pendant lights you’re likely to come across, with picks for modern minimalists, Belle Époque enthusiasts, and everyone in between.

First developed in 1958, the PH 5 is a classic for a reason. Renowned Danish lighting manufacturer Louis Poulsen designed the iconic pendant with a three-shade system to equally distribute light. While the pendant works for that functional purpose, it also doubles as an unconventional art piece. Choose from a number of monochromatic and multitone colorways, including this steel blue.

Look familiar? That’s because this large pendant, inspired by Noguchi’s Akaris, hangs in many an AD home tour, including above the dining room table in this Japandi-inspired Bay Area home and in the bedroom of this South African–inspired craft home, for example. There are a few reasons why this style of paper lighting is so popular: It goes with everything; it’s affordable; and it adds a sense of levity to the room with its ethereal shape and its soft, warm glow. As lantern pendants go, this option from Design Within Reach is about as low-commitment as they come. It works anywhere you want ambient lighting rather than a spotlight.

Designer pendant lights can cost upwards of $500, but you can pay a fraction of that with finds from Article, a direct-to-consumer furniture store that sells high-quality wares at more palatable prices. That’s certainly the case with the Aino pendant, with matte colorways and a simple angular silhouette that illuminates whatever you’re chef-ing up in your Scandi-style kitchen. The metal cone shade and braided fabric cord make it an easy pick for task lighting over a counter, and if you’re working with a tighter kitchen space, there’s also a mini pendant version for $99 that ships with an LED bulb.

This three-tiered ceiling light from West Elm adds visual texture to blank space, making it a welcome addition to transitional areas in need of personality. A piece like this can also help to add structure to an open plan where the kitchen and dining rooms collide. This piece comes in 26-inch and 32-inch sizes (the larger is a proper large pendant light that can hold its own over a dining table), as well as in hardwired and plug-in options, making it one of the few statement hanging lights you can install without calling an electrician.

Any cold kitchen space dominated by stone and steel fixtures could use a touch of handmade whimsy. Hanging this handwoven rattan pendant over a kitchen island promises to warm up the whole room. Usually, two or three pendant lights hang over a kitchen island; this single shade provides a cozy, welcoming alternative, softening the light rather than spotlighting the countertop, so your mise en place won’t feel so interrogated.

This midcentury Herman Miller pendant, aptly named a saucer for its elliptical shape, is prized by commerce writer Julia Harrison for its retro, Mad Men–esque appeal. Despite the resemblance to our Hay pick above, Miller wasn’t riffing on Noguchi with this pendant: He wanted a Swedish silk-covered pendant he couldn’t justify buying, so he made his own using a sprayed plastic skin originally developed for mothballing Navy ships (whereas the orbital Hay version uses rice paper). The vertical ribbing gives it a shape that seems to be almost expanding as it hangs. Its white shade and chrome hardware make it easily adaptable to many (if not most) design styles.

This lighting fixture brings to mind 10-foot ceilings, Flemish tapestries, and an echoey marble entryway. If you’re lucky enough to have all of the above, this is exactly the sort of pendant your space needs. If not, luckily, the fixture carries that whole vibe on its own, with its cream glass bowl, brass detailing, and a sculpted metal tassel that adds a bit of movement. A vintage-inspired piece like this transforms a more humble hallway into something dignified and scenic.

#5014. Crypto Card Provider Gnosis Pay Says Hackers Accessed Customer Data

Crypto Card Provider Gnosis Pay Says Hackers Accessed Customer Data

Crypto card provider Gnosis Pay said Friday that hackers accessed customer information through the system behind its own branded cards, the latest in a string of security incidents across the industry.

The company’s monitoring systems detected unauthorized access on Nov. 3, and containment began within two hours, Gnosis Pay said in a blog post. It has since replaced the compromised security credentials and fixed the vulnerability. Customer funds weren’t affected, the company said.

The breach was limited to Gnosis Pay’s consumer business, whose platform was already scheduled for retirement on Dec. 20. Its enterprise platform, which powers white-label card programs for fintech firms, wallets and neobanks, wasn’t affected.

Gnosis Pay didn’t say how many customers were affected or what data was exposed, but said it emailed each affected customer with details of what was taken from their account.

“While funds were not affected, the security of personal information is just as important and we take full responsibility,” the company wrote on X.

Gnosis Pay said it has stepped up monitoring of card activity and wallets for fraud, and has made 3D Secure, an extra verification step, mandatory for all online card payments until the consumer platform is retired. It has reported the breach to its partners, its card issuer and data protection authorities, and is arranging specialist monitoring for signs that the data is misused.

We had a data breach affecting the system powering Gnosis Pay-branded cards (our B2C business line) that resulted in the exposure of certain customer information.

Everyone affected was sent an email today with full information.

We deeply regret that this happened. While funds…

— Gnosis Pay 💳 (@gnosispay) November 6, 2026

The company urged customers to be wary of unexpected messages about their cards, even ones that include real personal details or purchases. It said it will never ask for seed phrases, PINs or full card numbers, or ask users to move funds.

It also told customers to be careful about sharing their address or details of their crypto holdings, both online and offline.

That last warning reflects a growing threat. Physical “wrench attacks” on crypto holders, including home invasions and kidnappings, were on pace for a record year after more than $30 million was stolen in the first half of 2026, according to blockchain analytics firm Chainalysis.

The disclosure follows several other recent examples of breaches in crypto security. Beyond the record-setting $6.4 billion hack of the world’s largest crypto exchange in Binance, rival trading platform Coinbase said last week that a breach at analytics vendor Amplitude exposed some customer contact data.

Last month, hardware wallet maker Ledger said all confirmed cases in a suspected $87 million drain involved devices bought from CryptoBilis, a Southeast Asian reseller, and urged recent buyers to move their funds to new devices.

#5013. What Rest-Pause Training Can Do for Your Workout

A simple practice lets you push past failure—without lowering the weight.

TRY AS YOU might, you can’t pump out reps forever when you’re working toward building bigger muscles. Eventually, you‘ll be too fatigued to complete another rep with good form, a point referred to as “technical failure.” Pushing beyond your last good rep can lead to sloppy lifting mechanics, which can compromise your results or, worse increase your risk of injury. If you find yourself stuck lifting the same weight for the same reps, it may be time to get creative with how you push past that iron hurdle.

One way to head off the siren song of ego lifting is by implementing a planned rest-pause set. It’s an intense lifting technique that requires focus and intention, and as effective as it can be, it shouldn’t be done for every set or even every exercise.

By briefly resting after reaching failure, you can squeeze out a few more quality reps using the same weight. A few extra reps might not seem like a big deal, but they can add up over time, giving your muscles an extra dose of the tension that drives growth.

To do a rest-pause set, you’ll take an exercise to the point where you can’t complete another rep with good form. You’ll then rest for five to 15 seconds—in one MH workout plan, fitness director Ebenezer Samuel, C.S.C.S. recommends taking “three deep breaths” for a pause—then squeeze out a few more reps (expect to be able to do two to four) using the same weight. You can repeat this process, though one additional round of reps is a good starting point.

If you rest longer than 20 seconds, Alexander Rothstein, an exercise physiologist and program coordinator of exercise science at the New York Institute of Technology, says you’re starting to approach a standard rest interval. "If you give yourself that brief moment to… recover, and then you continue the set, you haven't flushed away all of the waste products, and you haven't fully recovered your energy stores, but you are still capable of doing a few more high-quality reps,” he explains.

The purpose of a rest-pause set is to push your muscle to complete a few more hard (but high-quality) reps. If you’re aiming to build muscle, it’s those hard reps near the end of a set that are key; a rest-pause set allows you to sneak in a few more of those reps. While lifters of any experience level can perform rest-pause sets, they’re especially useful for advanced lifters who’ve already cashed in their newbie gains and find it increasingly difficult to progress their lifts and add muscle.

Say you’re doing the dumbbell bench press, an exercise on which you’ve been stuck using the same 50-pound dumbbells for sets of 8 to 10 reps for over a month. You get to that 10th rep and, once again, can’t muster another. Instead of calling it this time, you’ll sit up, rest the dumbbells on your thighs for 15 seconds, and then bust out another two reps. Congrats. You just eked out more worthwhile reps, which, over time, should help you progress.

“But isn’t that cheating?” you ask. No—and even if it were, there are times when cheating pays off for muscle gains. And research suggests that traditional sets and rest-pause sets can produce similar gains in strength and muscle. One small study had 18 experienced lifters perform six weeks of traditional sets or rest-pause training with the same relative load. One group completed three sets of six reps; the other did one set to failure and then as many rest-pause sets as necessary to hit 18 reps. Both groups achieved similar strength gains, but the rest-pause group experienced greater increases in thigh muscle thickness and leg-press muscular endurance.

As for how to start using them, pick one exercise per workout—ideally, a move you’re struggling to progress—and do the final set of that exercise as a rest-pause set. This often works best with the final exercise in your workout. The rest-pause protocol can be downright exhausting, and using them too early may compromise your ability to hit your typical reps and weights during subsequent sets.

One note: You can perform rest-pause sets with just about any exercise, but there are two places where they’re especially beneficial. Weight machines are the safest, letting you easily push to fatigue, then take your brief “rest”. Exercises that offer support for stabilizing muscles (think: chest-supported rows) also work well. And many single-joint free-weight exercises, like dumbbell curls are good options. If you’re going to attempt a rest-pause set with the bench press or back squat, have a good spotter on hand.

There’s more than one way to increase the volume of a single set, and these methods are often referred to as intensity techniques. Below, we’ll briefly compare the three most popular ones to rest-pause sets.

A drop set is when you reach failure on a lift, then immediately reduce the load (often by 10 to 20 percent) and try to squeeze out a few more reps. In many ways, it’s similar to a rest-pause set, but because you’re lightening the weight, you’ll find it easier to maintain good form.

“Personally, I just like the volume approach of drop sets,” Rothstein says. "When you do drop sets, obviously the load is being reduced, so it's not as potentially strength beneficial as it might be to just do rest-pauses and still lift heavy loads."

A cluster set is when you break up a traditional straight set into a series of mini sets, or clusters, with short rest periods between each. They're a popular training method among strength athletes, such as powerlifters and weightlifters, who want to accumulate more total tonnage.

Say, for example, you can typically deadlift 250 pounds for eight reps. You could load 285 to 300 pounds on the bar and perform four two-rep clusters, resting 15 to 30 seconds between, to complete the same eight reps with roughly 15 to 20 percent more weight.

The main difference between rest-pause and cluster sets is that cluster sets strategically use rest to achieve a preplanned number of reps, often at a higher weight. Rest-pause sets have you take a few breaths after reaching failure to push past what you can normally do rep-wise. Cluster sets are likely superior to rest-pause sets for building strength.

Instead of resting and then doing more reps once you reach failure, you can perform forced reps, where your spotter provides just enough assistance to help you complete a few more reps with good form. Forced reps are usually done on exercises where a spotter can easily help; think the bench press or certain machine exercises, not deadlifts or heavy back squats.

While you’re technically pushing past failure, you don’t have control over how much assistance your spotter provides; this can vary from workout to workout or even rep to rep. You can, however, control the eccentric portion of the contraction after the spotter has helped you, so if you’re doing forced reps, it’s a must to lower the weight slowly to get the most from these. Forced reps can be a useful occasional technique if you have an experienced spotter who knows how much assistance to provide; because they’re forcing you to acclimate to increased time-under-tension with a weight that you know is challenging.

Andrew Gutman, NASM-CPT is a journalist with a decade of experience covering fitness and nutrition. His work has been published in Men's Health, Men's Journal, Muscle & Fitness, and Gear Patrol. Outside of writing, Andrew trains in Brazilian Jiu-Jitsu, helps coach his gym's kickboxing team, and enjoys reading and cooking.

BTC/USD one-day chart with 50-week SMA. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

KOSPI one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

At an emergency meeting, Korea’s deputy prime minister and finance minister Lee Hyoung-il announced measures to reduce forced selling.

Brokerages will be exempt from a requirement to maintain a 140% collateral ratio on margin loans for three months from Monday, local media reported. Authorities said they would prepare futher measures if volatility increased.

“We recognize with gravity the expanding volatility in global stock markets following the U.S. midterm elections,” Chosun quoted an unnamed government source as saying.

Analysts linked Thursday’s Wall Street losses to fears of tighter AI regulation after Democrats won the House and Senate. Those fears compounded existing market concerns about the sector’s capital spending.

“In our view, a ban of AI will not happen in the US (with near-certainty). Such a ban, if even possible to enforce, would mean the US surrendering its position in the global AI race to China. That won’t happen,” it wrote in an X post.

#5011. 8 Best Online Framing Services for Displaying Art (2026)

Sometimes the most daunting part of getting art on your walls isn't tracking down a cool piece of art but finding the right frame for it. Generic online options tend to look cheap, and they’re offered in only the most basic dimensions. On the other end of the spectrum, a custom framer might quote you a higher price than the actual piece you’re framing. So, what’s an art lover to do? Luckily, there are a few tried and tested online companies that offer art gallery caliber frames at reasonable prices. Many take care of everything from printing to framing, with perks like complimentary hanging hardware to sweeten the deal. So if you’re ready to rescue those art prints still rolled up in a tube in your closet, scroll on to discover the online framing resources we trust with our precious works.

Framebridge’s offerings are more stylized than a lot of the other options you’ll find online, which is why it’s our top pick. Material options range from gilded bamboo to distressed whitewashed wood, with all frames supplied with matting, UV-protected acrylic, and hanging supplies. Either send in your artwork or upload a photograph for Framebridge to print at the desired size. “I'd been holding onto a few posters—a slick Valentino one and some vintage designs from a place in Tribeca I’m gatekeeping—when one of the brand’s numerous ads posed the answer to my framing woes,” says shopping writer Julia Harrison, who took her items to one of the brand’s physical locations (there are 44 across the country). After being walked through a different range of options and a month-long wait, Harrison told us the results were worth it. “I ended up ‘floating’ each piece I brought in—simple black frames with white or off-white backdrops for the first two, and a tortoise frame with gilded matte backing for the Robert Altman poster I brought in. I'm delighted with the final product—I feel like I'm, for once, treating my things respectfully.” The company can also frame objects such as commemorative buttons or sports jerseys that are less than 3/8" deep.

You might not have thought it, but Etsy is one of the most affordable solutions we’ve found for custom framing. If you’re looking to frame a piece of artwork or a poster you already own, many storefronts specialize in creating custom frames that will pair perfectly with your piece (just make sure your measurements are as accurate as can be).

Framers prove they know their stuff when the first question is exactly what you’re framing. Simply Framed starts with that—is it a canvas? An art print? A scarf? The company can even create a shadowbox or stretch a canvas within a frame and will recommend different options depending on the medium and size of the piece. #5010. US Sanctions 45 More Targets Tied to Alleged Crypto Scam Empire Prince Group

US Sanctions 45 More Targets Tied to Alleged Crypto Scam Empire Prince Group

The U.S. Treasury Department has sanctioned four individuals and 41 entities that it says support Prince Group, the Cambodian conglomerate accused of running scam compounds that use trafficked workers to defraud victims through crypto investment and romance scams.

The new designations, announced on Thursday by the Office of Foreign Assets Control (OFAC), target the offshore companies, nominee owners and administrators that Treasury says have helped Prince Group executives conceal assets and evade earlier sanctions imposed in October 2025 and June 2026.

"Cyber scam syndicates continue to steal billions of dollars from American victims while relying on sophisticated international business networks to launder their illicit proceeds," Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said.

Among those sanctioned is Qiu Yong, who Treasury said controls a network of British Virgin Islands and Hong Kong companies on behalf of Prince Group, including money lenders and wine businesses, and who has worked for a company controlled by Prince Group chairman Chen Zhi. His personal assistant, Zhao Mengyao, was also designated.

OFAC also targeted two people it said are holding property for, or running companies with, Prince Group executive Chen Bo, who was sanctioned in June. They include his wife, He Yujing, to whom Chen Bo transferred ownership of his Tokyo mansion after the October 2025 sanctions, according to Treasury, and associate Visal Pang, who controls 11 Cambodian companies spanning construction, logistics, a hotel and a "super app." Eleven companies still controlled by Chen Bo were also sanctioned, including Prince International Airlines and CB General Insurance.

Further entities in Singapore, Mauritius and Malaysia were sanctioned for links to Prince Group associate Dai An, along with a Singapore media company owned by alleged Prince Group finance chief Thet Li.

Separately, OFAC designated Chui Kok Wa, a Malaysian national based in Madagascar, who Treasury said led a network of scammers that used crypto and other technologies to defraud Americans of more than $400,000.

The U.S. is seeking the forfeiture of about $14 billion in Bitcoin tied to Prince Group, while UK authorities have frozen the London penthouse of Thet Li, Decrypt reported last month. Prince Group has denied the allegations.

Chen Zhi himself was arrested in Cambodia in January along with two other Chinese nationals and extradited to China, where state media described him as the head of a major cross-border gambling and fraud syndicate, the BBC reported. Cambodia, which had revoked his citizenship the month before, also placed the group's Prince Bank into liquidation.

Thursday's action was coordinated with the FBI, the Justice Department's Scam Center Strike Force, which has reported $580 million in crypto seizures and freezes, and the U.S. Attorney's Office for the District of Alaska, with help from authorities in the British Virgin Islands and the UK, according to Treasury.

Interpol declared scam compound networks a global threat last year, citing their use of human trafficking, online fraud and coerced labor. In September, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network linked about $12.7 billion in reported financial activity to suspected crypto investment scams, largely run by criminal organizations operating scam compounds in Southeast Asia.

The UN Office on Drugs and Crime estimated in July that scam operations across East and Southeast Asia, Australia and New Zealand cost victims between $88.3 billion and $114.1 billion in 2025, much of it through crypto investment fraud run from compounds holding people from at least 80 countries.

#5008. Bitcoin ETFs Bleed $430 Million as Tech Rout Tests Wall Street Buyers

Bitcoin ETFs Bleed $430 Million as Tech Rout Tests Wall Street Buyers

U.S. spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds lost $430.2 million to net withdrawals Thursday as a technology-stock selloff spread to crypto, with Fidelity and BlackRock leading the exits.

Fidelity's Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC) posted $199.9 million in net outflows and BlackRock's iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) shed $105 million, according to Farside Investors. Together they accounted for about 71% of the day's withdrawals.

Grayscale's GBTC lost another $49.9 million, while Franklin Templeton's EZBC shed $32.9 million. No fund in Farside's table recorded positive net flows Thursday, although several reported no net movement.

The latest withdrawals brought this week's net outflow to $869.8 million, following a $643.2 million outflow last Friday. Wednesday's modest $74.6 million inflow offered a brief reprieve before Thursday's reversal.

Fidelity's swing was particularly sharp: FBTC attracted $236.7 million Wednesday, then surrendered nearly $200 million the next session. BlackRock, meanwhile, lost $201.2 million even on Wednesday's positive day, extending a five-session withdrawal streak since October 30 to roughly $1.04 billion.

ETF flows have often been treated as a gauge of demand from investors accessing Bitcoin through regular brokerage accounts. The latest figures show that access works both ways: the same familiar wrapper that makes buying easy also makes exiting straightforward.

Because ETFs trade on stock exchanges, investors can adjust their Bitcoin exposure without transferring coins to a crypto wallet. That convenience can also make the funds an easy place to cut risk during an equity selloff. Daily net flows capture shares created or redeemed at the fund level, not every trade between investors.

Thursday's redemptions arrived as the Nasdaq Composite plunged 5.5% and the S&P 500 fell about 4.4%. Chipmakers and data-center stocks led the decline as investors questioned the pace of artificial-intelligence spending and potential restrictions after the U.S. midterm elections.

Bitcoin also surrendered its post-election rebound, falling back toward $76,000 after trading above $80,000 Wednesday. Short liquidations had helped power that rally, making the subsequent reversal more painful for leveraged traders.

The ETF figures add another piece to that picture. Net flows measure creations minus redemptions of ETF shares; they cannot tell whether holders were raising cash to cover losses elsewhere, trimming Bitcoin exposure, or rebalancing portfolios.

#5007. Binance hackers had 8-week intrusion window before $6.3B exploit: SEAL

The cybersecurity company flagged similar multi-stage intrusion patterns behind other high-profile crypto heists, including the $388 million Bitget hack.

Updated (Nov. 6 at 05:03 p.m. UTC): This article has been corrected to clarify the timeline of the Binance hack. The article also includes a statement from a Binance spokesperson.

Nonprofit cybersecurity organization Security Alliance (SEAL) estimates that North Korea-linked hackers compromised a Binance employee’s device about eight weeks before the exchange’s $6.3 billion exploit on Oct. 29, although Binance said the broader attack campaign began in June and that the hackers did not gain access to additional wallet infrastructure devices until Oct. 20.

The hacking group compromised a Binance software engineer’s device in September through a fake recruitment scheme, wrote SEAL in a Thursday report, adding that threat actors can navigate compromised systems with a “high degree of stealth over prolonged periods of time” after initial intrusion. SEAL’s eight-week estimate refers to the September-to-October intrusion window, rather than the beginning of the broader campaign described by Binance.

Cointelegraph contacted Binance for clarification on the timeline. A spokesperson said the initial device compromises occurred in June and September, but that attackers did not gain the access needed to reach wallet operations until Oct. 20.

“As outlined in our announcement, the initial device compromises occurred in June and September, but the attackers did not gain the access needed to reach wallet operations until October 20, when they used that foothold to move laterally and obtain access to additional devices and credentials,” the spokesperson said.

SEAL identified at least four “front companies” associated with TraderTraitor’s fake recruitment campaigns in 2026, including citalab.net, citalab.com, bitfit.io and oddium.io.

Binance traced the attack campaign back to June, when a separate employee’s device was compromised after the employee downloaded a tampered Homebrew installation while attempting to install an AI coding tool. The exchange detected the incident and replaced the device, rotating related credentials. However, the attackers had already obtained information about employees working on Binance’s wallet infrastructure, according to a Tuesday X post.

On Oct. 20, attackers used the compromised computer of the engineer targeted in September and an internal AI tool to gain control of two wallet infrastructure engineers’ devices, according to Binance. They subsequently discovered a deployment permission token, which was used during the Oct. 29 exploit.

The cybersecurity organization’s analysis pointed to similar multi-stage intrusion patterns in other high-profile crypto hacks. It estimated that attackers had about six weeks of access before the $388 million Bitget hack, six weeks of access to LayerZero before the vulnerability that led to the $293 million Kelp DAO exploit and about three weeks before conducting the $24 million AFX exploit.

The US Federal Bureau of Investigation attributed the attack to North Korea’s TraderTraitor, in a Saturday report. Kim Yo Jong, sister of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, dismissed the accusations.

#5005. Solana’s Shanghai event halted over unauthorized promotion: Report

Solana’s promotional campaigns through domestic entities allegedly violated China’s crypto-related restrictions, an unidentified PBOC official told China’s Financial News.

Chinese authorities halted the Solana Accelerate event in Shanghai over alleged unauthorized promotional activities involving domestic entities, China’s state-backed Financial News reported on Friday.

The outlet cited an unidentified official from a department of the People’s Bank of China (PBOC), who said Solana had promoted its brand through domestic entities, including by placing advertisements on a building in Shanghai’s Lujiazui financial district.

The official also claimed that Solana provides technical services supporting the issuance and trading of cryptocurrencies, which he described as illegal financial activities under Chinese regulations.

It marked the second reported disruption of a Solana event in mainland China in the past two years, highlighting how the country’s restrictions extend beyond digital asset trading.  An October 2025 Solana event in Shenzhen was also cut short over venue capacity and public-safety concerns, according to an X announcement by Solana’s Chinese-speaking community.

In an Oct. 16 X post, Solana Foundation president Lily Liu said organizers postponed the Shanghai event to “comply with local requirements” after an event registration unexpectedly quadrupled, exceeding the event cap pre-registered with local authorities.

In a February notice, the PBOC and several other agencies doubled down on crypto-related restrictions and included a new ban on the unauthorized issuance of yuan-pegged stablecoins.
