#5004. Paraguay Protests Over Bank Lockout Intensify as Impeachment Vote Nears

Paraguay Protests Over Bank Lockout Intensify as Impeachment Vote Nears

Thousands of Paraguayans marched through Asunción this week demanding their money back after Ueno Bank, a digital lender, locked about 3 million customers out of their accounts, according.

That is about 60% of Paraguay’s adults. The bank also held $800 million to $850 million in public money, including state pension and health funds, according to the AP, so the fallout reaches the pension system.

Anger boiled over Thursday night outside Ueno’s headquarters in Asunción, where savers burned debit and credit cards. Paraguayo “Payo” Cubas, a former presidential candidate, tried to join and was booed, shoved, and forced out.

Protesters shouted “divisor” and “oportunista,” meaning divider and opportunist, accusing him of trying to profit politically from public rage, according to the same report.

Diario Hoy reported that protesters also blamed him for the Senate’s current makeup, saying Cubas put Zenaida Delgado and Javier Vera on his party’s legislative ticket, the slate of candidates voters choose from, and that both joined the government’s side soon after taking office.

He then said he would face impeachment—a trial in Congress to remove a president—and defend himself.

The lower house had already voted 78-0 to open impeachment, and on Friday the Senate rejected his request for 72 extra hours to prepare his defense. The Senate planned a removal vote for Friday night, ABC Color reported, with Vice President Pedro Alliana next in line.

Horacio Cartes, the Colorado Party leader and Peña’s former political mentor, withdrew his support and backed the impeachment. “We will not be on the side of the plunder of public, private and retirees’ funds,” Cartes said.

Prosecutors also opened a criminal investigation into Peña’s possible responsibility for the bank’s irregularities, which he denies.

Police used water cannons and tear gas on marchers near Congress, and a new law requires most deposits to be returned within five business days, though protesters have not been placated.

The central bank says its deposit guarantee covers up to 228.3 million guaraníes per person, about $36,000, but as Decrypt previously reported, the first payouts will go only to accounts with up to 10 million guaraníes (around $1,600) and no debts to Ueno.

Bitcoiners, of course, rushed to use Paraguay’s banking crisis as an example on the benefits of decentralization and self custody, and why the “Bitcoin fixes this” motto is still relevant in today’s financial system. “Bitcoin adoption at a nation-state level can mitigate these types of situations,” said JAN3 founder Samson Mow. “If BTC is available to the people as a parallel rail, then systemic banking failures have far less impact.”

#5003. Ethereum devs won’t fork Glamsterdam on Hoodi until smart contract risks resolved

Ethereum developers are investigating concerns tied to eight smart contract invariants tied to Glamsterdam’s EIP-8037 and EIP-8038.

Ethereum core developers delayed the Glamsterdam upgrade’s implementation on the Hoodi testnet until security concerns tied to eight smart contract invariants are resolved.

Some existing Ethereum projects rely on assumptions that certain operations would cost a specific amount of gas, which would be invalidated by Glamsterdam’s EIP-8037 and EIP-8038 because they change how gas is charged for certain operations, the Ethereum Foundation said in a Friday blog post.

The decision followed an Oct. 13 report from smart contract auditor ChainSecurity, which found that the two Ethereum Improvement Proposals (EIPs) could break smart contracts that rely on fixed gas-related assumptions when external calls fail.

Delaying the Hoodi testnet fork gives developers more time to identify potential smart contract issues and determine whether changes are needed before Glamsterdam progresses toward mainnet deployment. The Hoodi fork will not proceed until the issues are resolved, added the Ethereum Foundation’s post.

Ethereum’s Glamsterdam upgrade went live on the Sepolia testnet on Oct. 6, in one of its final major rehearsals. The upgrade aims to advance Ethereum’s layer-1 scaling roadmap with enshrined proposer-builder separation, block-level access lists and changes to gas pricing that better reflect the cost of execution, the Ethereum Foundation announced in a Sept. 28 blog post.

#5002. The Man Behind Fashion’s Most Dazzling Spectacles Turns His Attention to the Art of Light

For as long as he can remember, Alexandre de Betak has been entranced by light. As a 10-year-old, he rewired his Paris bedroom so that visitors had to be buzzed in, greeted by shifting colored lights. He went on to found Bureau Betak, a set design agency that transformed the runway with its megawatt spectacles for clients like Dior, Gucci, Jacquemus, and Fendi. De Betak estimates that he produced “thousands” of fashion shows—including Saint Laurent’s spring-summer 2020 ready-to-wear extravaganza, for which he bathed the Eiffel Tower in golden light, and installations for Rodarte featuring fluorescent and neon tubes.

A new truss light sculpture by Alexandre de Betak, seen here in a 17th-century Swedish castle

Now, de Betak, who sold his company in 2021, is turning the spotlight on his own creative practice. On November 5, he opened his first solo exhibition of artworks at Salon 94 in New York. The electronically programmed Truss Light Sculptures are inspired by the scaffolding and fixtures of his early runway shows and in some cases literally made from them. “That equipment is old-fashioned now, but I wanted to honor it,” de Betak says. “And I liked the irony that I spent so much energy over the years trying to hide it.”

On view at Salon 94’s Upper East Side town house through December 19, the exhibit presents two series. In the gallery’s contemporary space, the first is composed of freestanding and wall-mounted aluminum structures fitted with LED bulbs. The upstairs Stone Room, a historic space with Caen stone plaster walls and arched windows, is the backdrop for a second series of versions in bronze. To make them, de Betak had a French foundry recast each part down to the smallest screw by hand, a process that sometimes required more than 20 molds.

A pair of wall-mounted sculptures in aluminum

The light sculptures, which vary from sconce-like creations to hulking constructions out of a sci-fi movie, have a sly sense of humor and a human quality. This is especially true when they come to life, thanks to a remote-control-operated system that activates light sequences that glow, sparkle, thrum, or react to ambient noise, “whether a whisper,” de Betak says, “or extremely loud music.” For these effects, he drew on his stage work, where he was renowned for cueing, or choreographing, light and sound. “I’m interested in the ephemeral nature of what this equipment was made to show—whether a fashion show or a ballet,” he says. “At a moment when everything is virtual, I think it’s more important than ever to create things that produce a live emotion.”

Salon 94’s founder Jeanne Greenberg Rohatyn observes that de Betak’s sculptures are equally powerful switched on or off. Unlit they remind her of David Smith artworks or Russian constructivism. Lit, and with synced sound, they evoke the theatricality of stage performers. “Alex has spent his career building structures that disappear into someone else’s spectacle,” she says. “Here, the structure is the whole point.”

#5001. Nigeria sets new crypto licensing rules, bans algorithmic stablecoins

Nigeria has banned algorithmic stablecoins and introduced reserve requirements for issuers as it expands central bank oversight of crypto payment services.

Nigeria’s central bank has introduced new licensing and reserve requirements for cryptocurrency companies, tightening oversight of stablecoins and digital asset payment services in one of Africa’s largest crypto markets.

The Central Bank of Nigeria (CBN) issued the Regulations for the Operations of Non-Security Virtual Assets on Thursday, establishing rules for stablecoin issuers, crypto payment providers, exchanges, custodians and other covered service providers.

The regulations took effect immediately, though existing operators have 180 days to apply for licenses or authorizations. The CBN said the framework aims to support responsible innovation while protecting consumers and financial stability.

The move follows President Bola Tinubu’s July executive order, which sought to coordinate crypto oversight among Nigerian authorities without changing their existing powers.

Under the new regulations, stablecoin issuers must fully back their tokens with reserve assets and maintain an additional buffer equal to at least 2% of their value. At least 40% of reserves must consist of cash or qualifying highly liquid assets.

The rules also set minimum paid-up capital of 10 billion naira ($7.4 million) for naira-backed issuers and 15 billion naira ($11.1 million) for foreign currency-backed stablecoin issuers.

Additionally, companies providing payment-related crypto services, including exchange and custody services, must maintain minimum paid-up capital of 1 billion naira ($742,000).

The regulations also prohibit stablecoins that rely primarily on algorithms rather than eligible reserves to maintain their value, along with tokens that lack enforceable redemption rights.

Nigeria has taken a restrictive approach to crypto trading, previously limiting banking access for exchanges and seeking to restrict peer-to-peer trading using local currency.

The country drew international attention in the crypto industry over its dispute with Binance, the world’s largest cryptocurrency exchange by trading volume.

Binance discontinued its Nigerian naira services in March 2024 amid government scrutiny of crypto trading and allegations of currency manipulation. Nigerian authorities also detained Binance executive Tigran Gambaryan for nearly eight months before dropping money laundering charges against him in October 2024.

More recently, Nigeria’s revenue agency introduced crypto tax collection rules for digital asset platforms and peer-to-peer marketplaces in August 2026.

#5000. Why a Hyperliquid Treasury Firm Wants to Become a US Futures Broker

Why a Hyperliquid Treasury Firm Wants to Become a US Futures Broker

Hyperliquid Strategies built its business around holding HYPE. Now it wants to help Americans trade on Hyperliquid, too.

The Nasdaq-listed treasury firm, which trades as PURR and is chaired by former Barclays CEO Bob Diamond, disclosed in its quarterly results Thursday that a subsidiary has applied to become a futures commission merchant, or FCM.

That’s the type of CFTC-registered broker that holds customer funds and handles futures orders. If approved, the unit would carry U.S. client accounts for permissioned markets built on Hyperliquid, letting Americans trade perpetual futures within a regulated framework.

Perpetual futures are derivatives with no expiration date that let traders take leveraged positions. Hyperliquid, a major onchain venue for them, still blocks U.S. users.

Its HIP-3 framework lets outside firms launch their own perpetual markets on Hyperliquid’s order book after staking HYPE. A new permissioned version, called HIP-3*, would let a regulated deployer run markets restricted to approved traders, with Hyperliquid acting as neutral infrastructure.

“We deployed $802 million this quarter and filed an application to become a futures commission merchant,” CEO David Schamis said. “Those two moves are the same bet.”

The firm held about 38.5 million HYPE at the end of September, worth roughly $3.5 billion, and more U.S. trading routed through Hyperliquid would likely mean more fees for the protocol, which uses fee revenue to buy back HYPE. An FCM would also give Hyperliquid Strategies a business of its own beyond holding tokens.

During the quarter, the firm spent $785 million buying 9.1 million HYPE and $17 million repurchasing 2.7 million of its own shares, funded largely by $911 million raised through stock sales. It reported $620.6 million in net income, driven almost entirely by $803 million in unrealized gains as HYPE rose from about $65 to $91.

It won’t be alone. Kraken parent Payward has said it plans to deploy HIP-3* markets through its CFTC-regulated Bitnomial exchange, with NinjaTrader Clearing serving as the FCM.

And last week, NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange agreed to team up with Trade[XYZ] to bring Hyperliquid-based perpetual futures to the U.S., sending HYPE up 13%. Trade[XYZ] already runs markets on Hyperliquid, including perpetual futures tied to the S&P 500.

Regulators must sign off on both the FCM registration and the permissioned HIP-3 markets it would serve, and the company has given no timeline.

#4999. What Democrats’ US midterm sweep means for the CLARITY Act

The CLARITY Act faces a new test as Democrats control Congress, raising questions over consumer protections, ethics and what the crypto industry may have to concede.

Crypto companies contributed $233 million to political committees during the 2026 US midterm cycle, according to Public Citizen’s analysis of federal filings.

Democrats won both chambers of Congress and will take control in January, giving them a chance to reshape the stalled CLARITY Act, a bill to set federal rules for crypto markets.

In Ohio, Democrat and longtime crypto skeptic Sherrod Brown defeated Republican Sen. Jon Husted despite crypto-backed political action committees spending $64 million to defeat him.

Senator Elizabeth Warren, one of crypto’s most vocal critics in Washington and the most senior Democrat on the Senate Banking Committee, is expected to chair the panel in the next Congress.

Stronger consumer protections, tougher scrutiny of political conflicts and a shift in regulatory power are all likely to be on the docket in the next round of negotiations.

But a Democratic majority doesn’t have to kill the prospect of crypto market structure legislation completely.

Democrat Congressman Shri Thanedar remains hopeful that the CLARITY Act can still pass, but says any legislation must include stronger ethics requirements. He tells Magazine:

Can the industry’s political influence still be converted into legislation, and what will it have to concede to get there?

The Senate failed to advance the CLARITY Act in September, with disagreements over ethics provisions contributing to the impasse.

The crypto spent $233 million on the 2026 US midterm elections. Source: Public Citizen

Democrats are already reportedly discussing how to reshape the market structure debate, with House Democrats said to be considering expanding proposals from Representative Maxine Waters into a broader market structure bill.

Related: Trump’s memecoin ventures derailed CLARITY Act, Scaramucci says

That bill could include stronger consumer protections, a greater role for the Securities and Exchange Commission, and new ethics restrictions on public officials’ crypto activities.

That could finally mean separating the industry’s push for clearer rules from the controversies surrounding Trump’s personal crypto ventures.

Democrats have already signaled plans to investigate the Trump family’s business dealings and other corporate interests, with incoming House Oversight Committee Chair Robert Garcia planning to pursue inquiries into corporate corruption.

Hadley Stern, head of institutions at Silence Laboratories and founder of Fidelity Digital Assets, says Democrats are right to “go after” the second, and the industry should want them to; that’s “what’s poisoning the bipartisan deal,” he says.

“A sitting president’s family launching memecoins and a stablecoin is a real governance problem,” he tells Magazine. “If I’d done that at Fidelity, I’d have been walked out the same afternoon.”

He says 2026’s digital asset industry is very different from the last time Democrats “held the gavel,” pointing to its growth from “FTX and a bunch of tokens” in 2022 to a federal law for stablecoins, Treasuries and credit funds onchain and tokenized equities on the way:

The industry may also have to accept more demanding standards if it wants legislation that can survive changes in political control.

Paul Brody, founder and CEO of Nightfall Networks and former global blockchain lead at EY, expects banks “to come out winners,” as they will “push for crypto companies” to be held to the same exacting consumer protection and security standards.

Related: Crypto Biz: CLARITY Act setback puts Coinbase in the spotlight

He says the industry has opposed this, but tells Magazine that “a wise strategy” would be to “accept similar conditions,” given that blockchain-native ecosystems are still “vastly more flexible, programmable and efficient.”

Lindsay Fraser, chief policy officer at Blockchain Association, also argues consumer protection and innovation should not be treated as competing objectives.

“We can protect consumers while ensuring the law reflects how the technology actually works,” she says, adding the goal is “lasting policy that earns public trust, gives builders a future in this country, and strengthens American financial leadership.”

That doesn’t mean it’s all smooth sailing to a potential compromise.

Brody says there’s still the possibility of a presidential veto, and points out that crypto is likely to be a “low priority” compared to “investigations of corruption and battles over budgets and the war.”

The midterm results came as lawmakers were already grappling with the fallout from the Binance hack, the largest hack in crypto history, with over $6.3 billion stolen on Oct. 29, raising fresh concerns about exchange security and customer protections.

Thanedar says while the legislation “wouldn’t solve every potential problem,” it would provide “much-needed certainty” and “security guardrails,” and the hack provides a “clear example of the danger of not enacting appropriate legislation.”

Maylea Ma, deputy general counsel at 1inch, says rather than making a stronger case for passing the latest draft of CLARITY, the incident “highlights problems the legislation does not address.”

The hack involved a “centralized exchange’s hot wallet and signing failure,” Ma says, but the current draft “has no insurer, no loss payee, and no hot wallet cap that would have mitigated the loss for Binance or imposed security standards that would have prevented the hack.”

Amanda Fischer, policy director at Better Markets, said the episode highlighted how “unfit for purpose the crypto ecosystem is for mainstream finance,” and crypto exchanges should not act as custodians, since it creates a “honeypot risk.”

Binance hack shows how ‘unfit’ crypto is for mainstream finance. Source: Amanda Fischer

Ma says the incident is “likely to impact” the next vote on CLARITY and could make lawmakers less willing to pass legislation the industry wants, unless it comes with stronger consumer protections.

Crypto spent years convincing Washington it deserves a seat at the table; now it will need to agree on the obligations that come along with it.

#4998. How to Organize a Kitchen Pantry in 6 Easy Steps, According to Experts

A pantry is among the most hardworking areas of the home, but without proper skills, it can also turn into the most cluttered. Learning how to organize a kitchen pantry like a pro requires a pro, so we tapped expert organizers to share their best pantry organization tips. Ahead, you’ll find a step-by-step guide to organizing your pantry—and keeping it that way. The process includes decluttering expired items, grouping pantry staples by category, deciding what to place on upper and lower shelves, and utilizing food storage containers and labeled bins. Whether you have a spacious walk-in pantry or you’re working with limited shelf space, use these pantry organization ideas to maximize vertical space, function, and style.

Start by removing everything from your pantry. “As you are taking the items out, take note of expiration dates and toss anything that is past its best by date, that is stale, or is no longer edible,” says Megan Barnhart, a professional organizer and founder of Barnhart Home. Since the pantry is rarely empty, take the opportunity to deep-clean it—wipe pull-out shelves, wash pantry storage vessels, and sweep the floor. You can also use this time to place liners or contact paper on the shelves. “Liners prevent damage to the wood and paint and make future clean-ups so much easier,” says Catherine Benson, a cleaning and organization aficionado based in Lexington, Kentucky.

Removing everything from the pantry will help you survey what you have in order to organize it better.

Before you return everything into the pantry, group items by category. There are several ways to do this, and experts recommend choosing the method that best suits you. One idea is to group like items together. “Think of your pantry as your own personal grocery store—canned goods together, pastas together, baking ingredients together,” Barnhart shares.

Another option is to categorize items by how you shop and plan meals. “If you shop with specific meals in mind and like to meal plan, group those ingredients together; for example, create a dinner zone that includes individual categories or bins such as pasta, grains, and soup,” says Lisa Eckerle, a professional organizer and owner of The Designer Organizer. If you shop by stocking up on pantry goods, categories like canned goods, baking supplies, and condiments may make more sense. “We normally use a hybrid approach for most of our clients, creating zones based on activity and categories within the zones based on food type,” Eckerle shares.

Once you’ve organized your categories, you might want containers to hold them. But before purchasing any storage vessels, measure pantry shelves, noting their width, depth, and height. Barnhart and Eckerle recommend incorporating the following storage solutions to maximize space and add style.

Risers are one of Barnhart’s favorite ways to store canned goods. “It makes it much easier to see the canned items in the back as well as what is in the front, and I feel it also helps avoid over-buying or repurchasing because you can see what you actually have,” she says.

Turntables are a must-have for pro organizers. “With all L-shaped pantries, we always start by placing turntables in most of the corners; these help maximize that space, avoid food getting lost or spoiled in the back, and create a cohesive visual,” Eckerle says. She notes they also work well on pantry shelves above eye level.

Consider containers and other tools to help store items in your pantry more effectively.

Turntables are great for oils and vinegars, sauces, condiments, broths, and spices. “If you have items you use daily, like nut butters, honey, and spreads that do not require refrigeration, consider putting those items on their own lazy Susan as well; it makes it easy to grab what you need and also makes it easy to clean up small spills and drips because they are contained to the lazy Susan,” Barnhart says.

Barnhart suggests using beautiful baskets or clear containers to group together things like snacks and dry goods. “This contains the items so they are not spilling out onto the shelves or floor and it is also much more aesthetically appealing as well,” Barnhart says.

One of Eckerle’s favorite recent hacks are wire cooling racks on the interior bottom of wooden storage bins. “By adding a plastic shelf liner under the cooling rack for easy cleaning, we created a unique fruit bin that was able to match the rest of the pantry’s decor and still store the fruit correctly with proper airflow,” she shares.

Now it’s time to add everything back into the pantry. “The most-reached-for categories should be near the door or center, with back stock and lesser-used category items placed in the back or the sides,” Eckerle says. She suggests putting meal-related items in the most accessible location, especially breakfast items, so they’re easy to grab when you’re in a rush.

Avoid storing seldom-used or heavy, small appliances on the top shelf or floor. “To prevent injury, damage, and frustration, those items should be stored between knee and shoulder height,” Eckerle says. Additionally, consider at what height you want each category. For example, “whether you want items out of reach of young children or dogs, or accessible and at eye level for children,” Eckerle says.

Keep things you grab often towards the front, while less-used items can go in the back.

“I am a big fan of labeling in the pantry—it helps systems stay in place when everyone knows what goes where and where to put it back,” Barnhart says. A label maker works great for this, but even stickers and sharpies will do.

Once you know how to organize a kitchen pantry, it’s important to maintain it. Eckerle recommends lightly resetting the pantry after every grocery haul. Additionally, she advises scheduling a day each quarter for a thorough pantry reset. “Re-check expiration dates, dispose of open food, and throw away or donate the items/categories that are no longer on your menu,” she says. You can also use this time to consider any seasonal menu changes that may impact how you organize your pantry. “Depending on the season, you may purchase different ingredients or cook different types of meals,” says Shweta Thota, a lifestyle content creator and Intel hardware engineer based in Sacramento. “This may require rearranging your pantry to accommodate seasonal items.” For example, in the winter, you may want to have a setup that works for more canned goods.

#4997. Greece Passes Crypto Tax Law With 10% Gains Levy, 15% on Staking and DeFi

Greece Passes Crypto Tax Law With 10% Gains Levy, 15% on Staking and DeFi

Greece's parliament passed a bill Thursday imposing a 10% capital-gains tax on cryptocurrency profits, marking a shift from a proposal discussed in October to legislation approved by lawmakers.

The measure also puts a 15% tax on income from staking, crypto lending and decentralized finance, or DeFi.

The legislation’s crypto provisions sit inside a wider Finance Ministry package dealing with debt servicing, financial oversight, and government accounting, with the new crypto income-tax rules scheduled for January 1, 2027.

Individual investors with annual net crypto gains of €500 ($557) or less will be exempt. Cross that threshold, though, and the 10% tax applies to the entire gain, rather than just the amount over €500 ($557).

For example, an investor with €600 ($669) in net gains would face a €60 ($67) tax under the new rate, not €10 ($11). The rules generally calculate gains by subtracting the acquisition cost from the value received when the crypto is disposed of.

Selling Bitcoin for euros is one taxable event, but spending crypto on goods or services can also count as a disposal. Investors will need transaction records to establish what they paid for their assets and what those assets were worth when sold or spent.

Yield from staking, lending, and providing liquidity to DeFi protocols is treated differently. Greece will classify those returns as interest income and tax them at 15%, according to the Finance Ministry.

Qualifying crypto losses exceeding €500 ($557) can be carried forward for five years and offset against future crypto gains. The bill also gives investors a 12-month window to voluntarily declare previously unreported crypto income without late-filing penalties or interest, provided they meet its conditions and pay within 60 days of filing.

The passage follows an October public consultation on the proposal.The draft proposed a 10% rate that was already lower than the 15% levy Greek officials had discussed in June.

Athens says exchanging one cryptocurrency for another should not itself trigger a capital-gains tax. But the wording lawmakers approved may not make that exclusion as clear as the government intended.

A review by parliament's Scientific Service flagged the bill's broad language covering disposals made "for consideration." That phrase could include token-to-token trades, the review said, even though the accompanying explanation says such swaps should not create taxable gains.

The reviewers recommended an explicit exemption and suggested clearer rules for exchanges involving stablecoins and electronic-money tokens. Those details matter for traders who rotate between assets without cashing out to fiat currency.

Greece is also preparing the administrative rules needed to put the tax into practice. A senior Finance Ministry official told Reuters that guidance on transaction transparency and tax audits would be issued in the coming weeks.

The government has not estimated how much the tax will raise. Officials say the market is difficult to measure because many Greek crypto investors use trading platforms based overseas.

#4996. 18 Best Leather Sofas of 2026: Couches That Are Editor-Approved

The best leather sofas adhere to the ABCs of great seating: agnostic design, beautiful texture, and classic silhouettes. Generally, genuine leather is lauded for its durability and longevity—it’s only going to look better with age and some patina. The fabric also makes a wildly comfy, easy-to-clean surface where dirt and dust can be wiped away with a cloth.

If you’re on the search for a new sofa that radiates sophistication (and not just single energy), there are a bounty of leather options at every price point, whether you prefer the softness of suede or the supple, lived-in feel of top-grain leather. Ahead, we gathered our favorite leather couches available online, from small-space designs to larger sectionals that will become the anchor for your future hangouts.

We also included a few wish-list-worthy collectible pieces from midcentury-modern makers like Tobia Scarpa and de Sede, in case contemporary silhouettes don’t quite measure up to the look you’re going for. Below, some of the most handsome leather styles on our wishlist.

Castlery Jonathan top-grain leather sofa is overstuffed, inviting, and supportive—the holy trinity of comfort. We love the inviting modular design that’s perfect for kicking your feet up on the nearby coffee table or simply lounging around in your modern living space. Rachel Fletcher, AD commerce director, has had the Castlery’s Jonathan sectional since 2022 and still gives it an A. The mixed-material sofa’s supple leather softens its industrial-looking stainless-steel foundation. An influential architect and designer who helped shape the 1950s and 1960s Italian design movement, Frattini’s ethos is apparent in this sofa, which reimagines traditional elements like tufting for modern tastes. If lounging is your main goal, extra padding on the already oversized arms eliminates the need for spare throw pillows, while the equally cushioned backrest makes it inviting for future movie nights.

The silhouette of this piece, a collaboration between furniture brand Four Hands and Los Angeles–based interior designer Amber Lewis, feels like an updated take on your parents’ bulky Chesterfield sofa. The built-in cushioning helps give the sofa a more elongated look and, while it could feel too formal for casual spaces, the front legs are fitted with tiny, charming brass casters that bring some unexpected levity to the silhouette.

While a lot of leather sofas look like clones of one another, this three-seater from Soho Home stands out among the pack thanks to its handsome wood frame and casually elevated feel. A shade like chestnut or sangria blend in more seamlessly with the oak wood for a more understated look, while the moss, honey, or classic black hues accentuate the soft edge frame to create a statement piece for your living room.

Not only is it available in various leather styles and a range of hues, but its pieces are detachable so you can rearrange them to align with your seating needs. Not feeling a three-seater anymore? Convert the set into a loveseat and an armless chair. The low slung sofa draws its design from 1970s influences, with oversized cushions and plush arms. Tailored flange seams add structure to the seams, achieving sophistication without lacking comfort.

Manufactured in Switzerland, the style is all about capturing mid-20th-century craftsmanship that lends itself to comfort. Retro vibes are not to be overlooked.

In a sea of black, brown, and tan leather sofas, Sixpenny’s curved Camino provides you with an opportunity to branch out with its playful “North Atlantic” shade of blue. At the same time, the durable, top-hide nubuck leather is well-suited to handle long naps and family movie nights.

For one, leather couches are durable as hell. Leather is said to last four times longer than a fabric sofa, not to mention the patina of aged leather is currently in vogue. It’s worth it: Durable leather furniture is going to stick with you for the long run. Plus, leather is hypoallergenic. Whatever type of leather you choose, no open fibers means no catching and holding dust or other allergens. (Take that, pet hair!)

If properly cared for, your leather sofa can last a good 15 to 25 years (or even longer)—just look at all the high-quality de Sede designs, Bellini configurations, and Togos on the vintage resale market. Bonded and faux leather sofas have a shorter lifespan (roughly three to seven years). Read on for more tips and pointers on proper leather care.

Dust your leather couch with a dry cloth regularly. You’ll also want to clean any spills or messes up immediately to prevent liquids or oils from sinking into the material. Just like our own skin, you should keep leather moisturized to maintain its vitalized appearance. Invest in a nice leather conditioner and give your furniture a good rub down once or twice a year to maintain its suppleness and prevent cracks, stains, and any other imperfections from worsening. For darker colored hides, keeping your sofa out of direct sunlight will also help resist fading. Read our explainer on how to clean leather furniture if you want to learn more ways to maintain your shiny new couch.

Believe it or not, there are many categories of leather. Full grain describes the most authentic version of the material—only the animal hair has been removed from the hide and has been naturally dyed. This type of leather is a bit tough at first, but it wears into a deliciously soft material after time. Top-grain leather is a popular option, as it’s been buffed to have a softer look (and feel) from the first sit. Split grain is a less expensive construction, as the first layer of leather has been removed. Bonded leather is another one you’ll see out there; it’s made from scraps of other projects and connected with an adhesive. No matter which one you choose, you’ll get that leather look and smell—it’s a great material for all budgets.

The world of leather sofas is expansive. With most furniture, we find successful decorating is less about the actual material and more about the silhouettes, which is certainly still the case here. Beloved for its versatility and durability, leather can evoke moodier interior vibes or more streamlined setups. Darker hues like black, chocolate brown, or navy blue signal the former—our forever muse Lenny Kravitz knows this better than anyone. Lighter tans, beiges, and taupes can feel cozy, like the vintage-looking couch we saw in this Brooklyn apartment with lots of sunlight. Since it’s one of the most popular upholsteries for modern furniture, the list of available sofa styles is endless: From classic midcentury-modern shapes with vintage appeal to softer, low profile designs that can outfit modern spaces like Devin Booker’s living room. In brighter hues, leather sofas can even be true statement pieces, like the textural scene-stealer in this Parisian apartment.

BTC/USD one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

US stock markets rebounded as traders took advantage of heavily discounted tech shares. The S&P 500 and Nasdaq Composite Index traded up 0.6% and 1.2%, respectively, at the time of writing.

Nasdaq Composite Index one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Certain tech stocks saw much sharper gains, with SanDisk and Micron Technologies both up 6.3%.

US civilian unemployment rate. Source: BLS

Stronger labor-market performance can create a headwind for crypto and risk assets as it suggests that the economy can manage any financial policy tightening, which in turn can reduce liquidity inflows to the market.

On the back of the latest data, expectations of the Federal Reserve hiking interest rates by 25 basis points at its December meeting increased from 66.9% to 76.8%, per data from CME Group’s FedWatch Tool.

Fed target-rate probability comparison for December FOMC meeting (screenshot). Source: CME Group

BTC/USD one-day chart with 50-week SMA. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

In his latest market analysis, Keith Alan, cofounder of crypto trading platform Material Indicators, warned of the consequences of losing the trend line, which has acted as support since mid-September.

“As strong as this support level looks from both a technical and structural perspective, we can’t confirm its durability before the W close,” he wrote on X, referring to the weekly candle close.

Alan saw a drop to as low as $69,000 for BTC/USD should the area around $75,500 fail to hold. A recovery, he said, depended on the pair producing a series of higher highs and higher lows on lower time frames.

#4994. Treasury Cuts Crypto Exchange MEXC Off From US Financial System

Treasury Cuts Crypto Exchange MEXC Off From US Financial System

The U.S. Treasury Department has moved to sever crypto exchange MEXC Global from the American financial system, accusing it of facilitating at least $16 billion in illicit transactions tied to Iran, North Korea and Russia.

In an order issued Friday through its Financial Crimes Enforcement Network, Treasury said MEXC, one of the world’s 20 largest crypto exchanges, served as a major conduit for sanctions evasion and cybercrime.

The action, part of a broader effort dubbed Operation Economic Outcast, bars U.S. financial institutions from processing any funds transfers to or from the exchange. It takes effect 30 days after publication in the Federal Register.

“Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system,” Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said in a statement, vowing to keep severing the regime’s “economic lifelines” and its attempts to use digital assets to dodge sanctions.

Founded in 2018, MEXC processes more than $1 billion in daily transactions, holds around $5 billion in reserves, and offers more than 3,000 tokens, according to FinCEN. Between May 2021 and May 2026, FinCEN said, the platform moved at least $16 billion connected to illicit activity, including nearly $1.5 billion tied to Russian sanctions evasion, with $232 million in direct transactions with sanctioned entities. The agency named MEXC a “primary money laundering concern” in connection with Russian illicit finance.

Despite publicly claiming anti-money-laundering controls and barring users in places like Iran and North Korea, MEXC’s compliance was riddled with “severe deficiencies,” FinCEN said.

The exchange was still transacting with sanctioned Russian exchange Garantex and its successor Grinex as recently as June 2026, more than four years after Garantex was first designated. It also processed roughly $200 million linked to the A7A5 stablecoin and about $22 million benefiting Iran’s central bank and its Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps.

Those connections place MEXC squarely within a sprawling sanctions-evasion ecosystem that Treasury has been dismantling piece by piece.

The department last month designated Russia’s A7 Network a transnational criminal organization and proposed cutting its front companies off from U.S. finance, flagging more than $179 billion in transactions through its ruble-backed A7A5 token, much of it routed through Garantex and Grinex.

The same campaign has reached into Iran’s crypto sector, where Treasury gained sweeping authority to sanction anyone operating in it and blacklisted the Iranian exchange Nobitex over its role in funneling crypto to the IRGC.

The order uses a provision of the Combating Russian Money Laundering Act rather than a standard sanctions designation, a tool FinCEN has increasingly reached for to cut off crypto infrastructure that operates outside the correspondent banking system.

#4993. Strive buys 15,124 Bitcoin in October, closes in on top treasury holders

Bitcoin treasury company Strive reported $509 million in third-quarter net income and purchased 15,124 BTC in October, bringing its total holdings to 43,124 BTC.

Bitcoin treasury company Strive purchased 15,124 Bitcoin (BTC) in October, bringing its total purchases for the first 10 months of 2026 to 35,497 BTC, according to its third-quarter earnings report released Friday. The purchases brought the company closer to the top of the corporate Bitcoin treasury rankings.

Strive reported that it held 43,124 BTC as of Oct. 30, making it the fourth-largest publicly traded corporate Bitcoin holder, according to BitcoinTreasuries.NET data. It trails third-place Twenty One Capital by just 390 BTC and would need another 877 BTC to surpass second-place Metaplanet, which holds 44,000 BTC, assuming neither company increases its holdings.

Strive’s accumulation follows comments made by CEO Matt Cole in a September interview, in which he said the company could become the world’s second-largest publicly traded corporate Bitcoin holder by year-end, although he acknowledged that the outcome was not his base-case scenario.

Strive’s Bitcoin accumulation over the past month. Source: BitcoinTreasuries.NET

Strive also raised approximately $609 million in net proceeds through an October offering of its SATS perpetual preferred stock, which carries a 15% annual dividend rate. The company has not specified how much of the proceeds it will allocate to Bitcoin purchases.

Strive reported $509 million in third-quarter net income, driven largely by $533.7 million in unrealized Bitcoin gains.

According to its earnings report, Strive is also evaluating opportunities in banking, brokerage, decentralized finance, reinsurance and tokenization as part of a broader strategy to leverage its balance sheet.

Jeff Walton, Strive’s chief risk officer, told Cointelegraph that the company is looking beyond cryptocurrency markets for ways to put its Bitcoin holdings to work.

“We believe that some of the bigger opportunities are crossing the aisle into traditional finance, operating as a structured finance company, and finding yield and distribution that is not correlated to Bitcoin or crypto,” Walton said.

Shares of Strive (ASST) fell 1.97% to $20.19 in early trading Friday, extending their decline over the past month to 32.15%, according to Yahoo Finance data at the time of writing.

#4991. Russia finalizes crypto trading rules ahead of Nov. 17 rollout

The rules allow retail investors to trade BTC, ETH and USDT through regulated intermediaries, with Moscow Exchange planning to launch crypto trading in December.

Russia’s central bank has finalized rules allowing exchanges to offer Bitcoin (BTC), Ether (ETH) and Tether USDt (USDT) to retail investors starting Nov. 17.

Under the rules, non-qualified investors can purchase up to 300,000 rubles (around $3,700) in cryptocurrency annually through each intermediary, including brokers, asset managers and crypto exchange providers. Qualified investors face no purchase limits, but all investors must pass a mandatory test before trading.

The directive builds on legislation signed by Russian President Vladimir Putin on Aug. 4 establishing a regulated cryptocurrency market under central bank oversight.

A week later, on Aug. 11, the Bank of Russia published its draft regulations, identifying BTC, ETH and USDT as eligible for public trading based on market capitalization, average daily trading volume and at least five years of price history on foreign exchanges.

The law took effect Sept. 1, and the Bank of Russia subsequently authorized Sberbank, the country’s largest bank, to offer crypto exchange services in October.

The regulatory developments come as Moscow Exchange, Russia’s largest securities exchange and a publicly traded company, prepares to launch cryptocurrency trading on Dec. 1, initially offering the three cryptocurrencies through pilot transactions.

While expanding access to cryptocurrencies through regulated platforms, Russia is also tightening restrictions on transactions outside those channels.

Direct peer-to-peer crypto trading will be prohibited starting July 1, 2027, while transfers from regulated digital accounts to self-custody wallets are already banned, according to the Bank of Russia. The country also maintains a prohibition on using cryptocurrency to pay for goods and services.

#4990. Sony Axing Game Discs Will Have Outsized Impact on Low-Income Gamers, Experts Say

Sony Axing Game Discs Will Have Outsized Impact on Low-Income Gamers, Experts Say

Gamers in low-income areas will be among the hardest hit by Sony’s decision to stop producing physical game discs from 2028, experts told Decrypt, while one African games industry expert arguing the continent stands to lose out badly.

The secondary market has long been a cheap way for gamers to get hold of games. The PlayStation Store does run sales, but buying digitally assumes a decent internet connection, which rural households are less likely to have.

"By abandoning physical discs, some players at the bottom of the economic spectrum lose access to physical distribution through retail and, more importantly, discounted used sales," Joost van Dreunen, professor at the NYU Stern School of Business and video game business author, told Decrypt. "It won't immediately show up in the financials of major game makers, since their focus is now on creating premium experiences for a luxury class of gamers."

It comes as the cost of being a gamer is trending upwards. Sony raised PS5 prices in March, for example, citing "pressures in the global economic landscape." Similarly, the next generation of Xbox is expected to cost up to $1,800, according to Fortune.

A spokesperson for UK advocacy group Gamer's Voice told Decrypt that Sony's move to cut discs will "likely affect people on lower incomes more."

Isa Muhammad, founder of Africa and Middle East-focused games industry platform Bijetic, told Decrypt that the PS5 is already "out of reach for most Africans." He stressed that those who can buy a console still rely on the second-hand games market to afford to play on the consoles.

Well, at least the highest-income Africans could still take advantage of the PlayStation Store discounts, right? Unfortunately not. Muhammad said that the PlayStation Network is only supported in one African nation: South Africa. Many other nations are missing from the PlayStation Network's official list of supported countries.

"A gamer in Nigeria who wants to buy digitally has to set up an account registered in the U.S. or UK, then pay with foreign gift cards or a dollar card," Muhammad said.

Download speeds in rural, remote, and low-income areas are another major hurdle.

"Bandwidth is now part of what a game costs," van Dreunen said. "Roughly one in four rural [American] households has no wired provider offering 100/20 Mbps, and at 10 Mbps a 50GB download takes about 11 hours."

In Africa and the Middle East, Muhammad said, internet speeds vary significantly across the region—from super-fast connections in Dubai to unreliable ones in Iran. Internet access is also costly in some countries.

"Just recently I paid 75,000 NGN (around $55) for a 450GB broadband bundle, which is more than Nigeria's minimum monthly wage," Muhammad told Decrypt. With GTA 6's pre-load file expected to be 212GB, "a gamer here would first spend $80 on the game, then anywhere from $25 to over $60 just to download it, depending on how they buy data."

And at Nigeria's median download speed of 36.77 Mbps, per Speedtest by Ookla, the download would take nearly 13 hours. Not to mention the fact that they'd need to have a non-Nigerian PlayStation account, a download code corresponding to the country their account is supposedly from, and a way to pay in the local currency.

"Sony's own terms say you must register your account in the country where you live, and Sony can suspend or close accounts created with false information," Muhammad told Decrypt. "So every Nigerian or Egyptian on a US or UK PSN account is breaking the rules from day one."

African gamers live in fear of needing help from Sony and being refused it (or worse, banned) because their accounts break its rules, Muhammad explained. In a future where every purchase is digital, it could open the door to gamers losing accounts that hold every game they've ever purchased.

"If a player in Africa buys GTA 6 and can't recover their account, they may have no choice but to buy it again on a new one, along with everything else they've lost," he said. "A disc-free future will see this become the reality for every new PlayStation game, for gamers across most of Africa."

Sony and PlayStation did not respond to Decrypt's request for comment.

#4989. US 401(k) proposal leaves door open to altcoins, but hurdles remain

While the proposal doesn’t exclude altcoins and smaller cryptocurrencies, experts say they could face challenges getting into retirement plans.

Smaller cryptocurrencies could become available in US 401(k) retirement plans under a proposed Labor Department rule, but experts say they may face more challenges than Bitcoin (BTC) and Ether (ETH).

The rule reached the White House for final review on Thursday, according to the Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs. It aims to make it easier for retirement plans to offer alternative investments like crypto, while offering more legal protection for employers and plan managers.

The Labor Department’s March proposal neither specifies which digital assets could be included nor sets a minimum market capitalization requirement. However, it does guide retirement plan managers to consider six factors when choosing: performance, fees, liquidity, valuation, benchmarks and complexity.

The proposal could open the door to more cryptocurrencies in retirement plans, however, it does not guarantee they will be offered. And when it comes to smaller tokens, experts say they could face challenges because of their limited history, liquidity and other risks.

Teresa Goody Guillén, managing partner at Bellementis PLLC, said Bitcoin and Ether are likely to be the first cryptocurrencies offered because of their established markets and investment products. Bitcoin and Ether are the two largest cryptocurrencies by market capitalization, at approximately $1.5 trillion and $273 billion, respectively, according to CoinGecko data as of 20:01 UTC on Nov. 6.

Still, that doesn’t automatically exclude altcoins, she said. Instead, those managing 401(k) plans would need to show that their investment choices are sensible.

“The real question isn’t which cryptocurrencies regulators pick – it’s whether an investment meets the same standards of prudence, liquidity, valuation and investor protection as any other retirement option,” Guillén said.

She added that while a digital asset’s market cap matters, it shouldn’t be the deciding factor. “Big doesn’t mean suitable, and small doesn’t mean disqualified,” Guillén said. “Digital assets shouldn’t get special treatment, or be excluded because they use different technology.”

Bernardo Brites, co-founder and CEO of Trace Finance, echoed Guillén sentiment, adding: “Run any token through that list and the answer is short. Bitcoin passes clearly. Ether passes; everything else fails on track record or benchmark before a fiduciary ever gets to market cap.”

Brites explained, in this situation, market cap matters only indirectly. “The practical test is simpler: can a plan committee document a daily, reliable price from a regulated wrapper?” he said. “If not, it is not going in.”

While the Labor Department’s proposal does not limit crypto investments to exchange-traded funds (ETFs) or managed funds, experts said those products will likely be offered before direct crypto holdings.

“ETFs and managed funds will likely come first because they fit existing custody and recordkeeping systems,” Guillén said. Whereas holding cryptocurrencies directly would raise additional concerns about storage, cybersecurity and pricing, Guillén said. Still, she added that those risks should not automatically rule them out.

Brites also expects crypto investments to become available gradually. He said existing brokerage options would likely come first, followed by standalone ETFs and funds that include small amounts of crypto.

He expects some crypto investment options to become available in 2027, followed by ETFs at larger employers later that year. Crypto could become more widely available in retirement funds by 2028, he said.

“The rule changes the legal risk, not the plumbing, and the plumbing is slower,” Brites said. “White House review can take up to ninety days, then publication, then an effective date typically sixty days later.”

Brites further explained that legal uncertainty could also slow adoption. The proposal would offer retirement plan managers some protection from lawsuits, but it would not prevent them from being sued over their investment decisions.

He also warned that future administrations could reverse the rule, a possibility that could make employers hesitant to add crypto to long-term retirement plans.

“The 2022 guidance was reversed in 2025; a fiduciary signing a ten-year plan design has to consider that this rule could be reversed too,” he said. “And with courts giving less deference to agencies, how much weight the safe harbor carries in litigation is itself unsettled.”

#4988. Billy Joel at Home: 10 Photos of the Piano Man Through the Decades

For Billy Joel, New York is more than just a place. It’s an inextricable part of what makes the singer who he is. “My speech patterns are New York. The music that I like, it was all New York–based. That’s what I think of when I think of home,” he said in his 2025 HBO documentary Billy Joel: And So It Goes. “It’s my roots.”

The Bronx native grew up in Hicksville, a middle-class suburb of Nassau County on Long Island. His family struggled to make ends meet. “There was poor, and then there was Billy Joel poor. He was lucky if he had enough money to buy a pack of cigarettes,” his childhood friend and fellow musician Jon Small said in And So It Goes. According to Joel’s sister Judy, no matter how hard times were, their mother Rosalind insisted he take piano lessons. His talent “had to be nurtured,” Judy said in the HBO doc. “She knew that he was going to be something someday.”

Before he indeed became something, Joel worked on an oyster boat. “I used to look up at this mansion on the hill and wonder what it would be like to live in a house like that,” he said in the documentary, referencing the waterfront property on Centre Island he nicknamed MiddleSea. “I used to think, ‘They’re rich bastards. They never had to work a day in their life.’ Well, I own that house now. It’s not finished yet. And neither am I.”

Read on for a glimpse into the Grammy winner’s life, from his early days in New York to his brief stint in Los Angeles and return home.

Joel’s music career took the Long Island native to Los Angeles in the early 1970s, and he signed with Columbia Records in 1973. After three years in LA, he and his first wife, Elizabeth Weber, returned to his home state, where he completed his fourth studio album, Turnstiles. “I wanted to get back to New York and make more of a rock and roll album,” Joel explained in And So It Goes. “I was on a bus on my way back to Highland Falls, New York. Elizabeth found us a house up on the Hudson River, and it was fall. It was just gorgeous. The colors were ablaze, the trees were, like, gold and red, and I remember thinking, ‘This is the New York State that I loved when I was a kid. This is magic. We don’t have this in California.’ And I wrote this love song to New York on the bus.” It would become “New York State of Mind,” one of Joel’s most popular tunes.

The singer-songwriter poses with some of his records in January 1978.

Joel’s fifth studio album, 1977’s The Stranger, was a runaway success—but the singer, pictured alongside his accolades in New York in 1978, told Playboy a few years later that his newfound fortune didn’t faze him. “I read these stories about Peter Frampton’s multimillion-dollar mansion, 15 Rolls-Royces and all that,” he said. “Who cares? It’s all bullshit. What was his last record like? Was it any good? That’s the question. People get hung up on the money thing. It’s nice to have four motorcycles. It’s fun. And I walk into the garage and go, ‘Holy shit! I’ve got four motorcycles!’ But I don’t go beyond a certain point.”

Joel, his then-wife Elizabeth, and their dog in 1978.

Instead of splurging on a million-dollar mansion, Joel told Playboy that when he and his wife decided to buy a home, “We got a $300,000 house with a mortgage like everybody else.” Their glass house, featuring floor-to-ceiling windows overlooking the Long Island Sound, was located in the town of Oyster Bay on Long Island’s North Shore. Joel appears poised to hurl a rock at the home on the cover of his 1980 album, Glass Houses. “The old thing about people who live in glass houses shouldn’t throw stones—I don’t believe it,” he said. “I think, Why not? Take chances. I really wanted to throw a rock at my own house.”

Joel and Elizabeth at their Long Island residence.

In the 1970s, Joel asked Weber to be his manager. “The company I’m with now is New York–based,” Joel said of his representation. “It’s called Home Run—nothing to do with baseball. It’s a home-run agency.” Weber got right to work. She recalled in his HBO documentary that the “story [Joel] likes to tell” of her early days on the job is that he woke up the next morning to phones everywhere and “cabinets installed.” However, she said, “That’s not really true, but it’s close to true.” The couple (seen here at Joel’s grand piano in their living room) split time between their Manhattan apartment, where they worked, and Oyster Bay. Weber left her role managing Joel when the two split, and her brother Frank picked up where she left off. Home Run dissolved amid financial woes in the ’80s, and Joel eventually pressed charges against Frank over mishandled funds; the parties reached a settlement, the terms of which have not been disclosed publicly.

The musician mixing tapes in the music studio of his New York home.

The Oyster Bay house, a midcentury-modern residence, was not far from where Joel grew up in Hicksville. Photographed here in his at-home music studio in 1978, the singer’s career was on the upswing—but his relationship with Weber was growing tense. “Having a big boom in record success, I think my marriage was starting to unravel around then; 1979, it wasn’t the happiest time,” Joel once said. “Record success doesn’t translate into happiness.” Joel assumed things were paying off financially, but he admitted he “wasn’t aware” how much money he was actually bringing in. “I know we were living in a nice house, my wife was going out and buying all kinds of nice stuff,” he said. “I never really went out and did that.”

Joel multitasks in his LA home studio in 1984.

Though Joel had settled down on the East Coast, he told the Los Angeles Times that his California years cross his mind “all the time.” The singer, pictured here in LA in 1984, wrote his hit song “Piano Man” based on his experience as a lounge musician (using the name Bill Martin) at a now-defunct bar near the corner of Wilshire Boulevard and Western Avenue during the early 1970s. “I wonder what happened to the people I knew—the people in the bar in ‘Piano Man,’” he said. “It was the Executive Room in the Wilshire district. I got tips and made union scale, and I was able to afford an apartment in Studio City. The weather was great; the people were nice.”

Though it appears Joel lived like a rock star in Los Angeles—as seen here in 1984, perched on a sheepskin-style throw over a leather sofa with a pack of Marlboros and a landline telephone by his side—he told the Los Angeles Times in 2023 that he felt more like “an exiled writer living in Paris” during his early stint in the City of Angels. “I went from being an insignificant nobody to the Piano Man while I was there,” he said. “I still look back to my time in LA with a certain amount of romance.”

Joel is pictured here at the dinner table in 2001. When The New Yorker paid him a visit at his Centre Island residence in 2014, he described the interior’s aesthetic as “Catholic martyrs and saints, grotesqueries.” In the library, Joel—who decorated the place himself—had a model of the Queen Mary on his mantel, and in the living room facing the fireplace was a triptych of the Last Supper along with silver jugs and bowls on a nearby table.

Joel’s various properties have passed through some famous hands. He and his second wife, supermodel Christie Brinkley, owned a 12-acre oceanfront property in the East Hampton hamlet of Amagansett. The couple raised daughter Alexa Ray Joel in the Long Island dwelling. It was later sold to Jerry and Jessica Seinfeld for $32 million. According to The New Yorker, Joel also sold a duplex on Central Park West to singer-songwriter Sting for $5 million and a house on Martha’s Vineyard to businessman Mickey Drexler. At one point, Joel purchased a home in the Hamptons from Jaws star Roy Scheider.

“As a little kid, I always liked boats and the romance of living on an island,” Joel told Parade in 2018. The singer—pictured here on an East Hampton dock in 2001—has always felt at home on the water. In 2007 he designed a 57-foot powerboat called the Vendetta that can go about 50 knots, per The New Yorker. Boating represented “freedom,” Joel said in his documentary. “I am the captain of my ship.”

#4987. AI Chip Stocks Bounce After Global Rout—But Is the Bubble Starting to Burst?

AI Chip Stocks Bounce After Global Rout—But Is the Bubble Starting to Burst?

AI stocks staged a comeback Friday after a rout that swept from Wall Street to Asia. But a single green session hardly settles the bigger question for investors heading into the weekend: Is this an overdue correction, or the beginning of an AI bubble bursting?

Nvidia rose 2.78% to $186.64, after plunging 9.3% this Thursday. AMD also bounced to $449.04 following a 17.3% collapse a day earlier. Both stocks remain well below their October peaks, showing how little a sharp relief rally can repair.

It wasn’t just U.S. markets seeing red either. South Korea’s KOSPI lost 6% Friday, while Taiwan’s benchmark dropped more than 8% during the week—its worst week in months. TSMC hit its daily limit-down threshold during the rout. Reuters reported that Taiwanese officials asked state-owned banks to help support prices.

The US Tech 100 cash-market proxy also recovered, gaining 1.85% to 27,007.3, which means it’s down more than 5.5% this week. Its daily candles show the bounce arriving after a much larger slide from October levels, not a return to the previous trend.

The U.S. midterms accelerated fears that Democratic gains would mean tougher scrutiny of data centers. In 40 of 41 local ballot contests on November 3, voters favored limiting development, including by rejecting pro-construction measures, Ballotpedia found. Some votes were advisory, but the political message was unmistakable.

That matters for chip companies because data centers are their biggest growth story. Each project requires vast spending on processors, servers, power and cooling. Restrictions could delay orders or force construction elsewhere.

Yet the doubts predated the election. AI companies and their customers are spending aggressively to build capacity, often before returns are clear. More expensive borrowing makes that bet harder to sustain. Reuters quoted market strategist Steve Sosnick warning that heavily owned stocks were already “priced to perfection.”

A correction means a market has fallen at least 10% from a recent peak. It does not necessarily mean the underlying business is shrinking. Imagine a chipmaker whose shares trade at 60 times annual earnings because investors expect explosive growth. If investors become willing to pay only 40 times those same earnings, the stock can lose a third of its value without profits falling at all.

A bubble bursting is more serious: It happens when expectations prove unrealistic and the money supporting the boom begins to disappear.

History offers a useful warning. During the dot-com crash, the Nasdaq lost roughly 77% between March 2000 and October 2002. The internet was not a fad, but investors had vastly overestimated how quickly companies could turn it into profit.

Telecom operators spent fortunes laying fiber-optic cables for customers who had yet to arrive. Global Crossing, which built an international network, filed for bankruptcy in 2002. The technology survived; many companies and shareholders did not.

AI could face a similar mismatch between long-term promise and near-term returns—and some investors are already talking about it. For example, the RAM shortage is also a business opportunity that many companies are trying to use for their advantage. Former crypto miners are renting their hardware and cloud provider and inference startups are appearing everywhere.

Now, consider a hypothetical company borrowing billions to build a data center packed with Nvidia chips, expecting to rent that computing power at premium prices for years. If customers spend less than anticipated, borrowing becomes more expensive or construction is delayed, the debt still has to be repaid.

The operator may cancel chip orders, its contractors lose work, and utilities that planned new capacity lose anticipated customers. Lenders and investors holding its debt could also take losses. A stock-market selloff can then become a problem for the wider economy.

There is an important difference from 2000, however. Microsoft, Alphabet, Amazon and Meta are profitable businesses with established customers and substantial cash flows, unlike many of the speculative dot-com startups. They can withstand setbacks better than smaller AI-cloud providers that depend on repeatedly borrowing to expand.

It is possible for AI to keep transforming industries while a bunch of individual AI companies fail—and for investors in successful companies to have overpaid.

The financial warning lights are beginning to flicker. Reuters reported Friday that the cost of insuring against a default by AI-cloud provider CoreWeave had climbed above 1,000 basis points. Such protection works somewhat like insurance on a loan: as the price rises, investors are signaling greater concern about repayment.

Yet broader bond markets were still operating relatively normally. That points to growing strain in the riskiest corners of AI financing, not proof of a system-wide crisis.

Next week will offer more concrete tests than another day of red or green candles. Results from CoreWeave, Applied Materials, and Cisco could reveal whether customers are still ordering capacity and equipment, while inflation data will influence expectations for borrowing costs. Stable orders and manageable debt would support the correction argument.

Repeated canceled projects, failed refinancing and shrinking sales would suggest the boom's financial foundations are cracking. The question is not whether AI has a future, but whether every company betting billions on that future can afford to get there.

#4986. Binance hack is ‘cyber war,’ TRM Labs executive says

TRM Labs executive Ari Redbord called for governments to take offensive action against North Korean hackers following the record $6.3 billion Binance theft.

The $6.3 billion Binance hack sent shockwaves through the crypto industry. Following a series of increasingly severe cybersecurity incidents, the attack on the world’s biggest crypto exchange demonstrated how far state-backed hackers can, and will, go.

TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence company, was on the front lines of efforts to stop the theft, releasing a report in its aftermath. The firm was early to attribute the attack to the North Korean hacking group, TraitorTrader, citing “indisputable” onchain and offchain evidence. The $1.5 billion hack of Bybit in February 2025 was attributed to the same group.

Binance said in its postmortem on Nov. 3 that attackers posed as recruiters to plant malware on an engineer’s work computer, then used a compromised internal AI tool to reach its wallet-signing service.

Ari Redbord, TRM Labs’ global head of policy and government affairs, told Cointelegraph that although the industry’s coordinated response to the attack was unprecedented, “we are only going to see much more of this.”

The speed and scale of industry’s response to the hack were among the factors that stood out to Redbord.

Tether froze 971.1 million USDt (USDT) tokens just 37 minutes after the main sweep. Combined, stablecoin issuers froze tokens worth about $2.67 billion, according to TRM. “Nothing like that has ever been done before,” Redbord said. “They were able to claw back 40% right out the gate.”

The race to catch stolen funds before hackers can move them offchain is a critical part of defending against such attacks. TRM’s Redbord explained that the Beacon Network, an intelligence-sharing system TRM launched in 2025, allowed companies to coordinate quickly to immobilize the funds before working to seize them. “Normally I see a few Beacon alerts maybe every day. I’ve seen, I don’t know, hundreds or maybe even a thousand Beacon alerts over the last few days,” he said.

Related: Ethereum devs won’t fork Glamsterdam on Hoodi until smart contract risks resolved

The push for coordination comes as Bloomberg reported on Nov. 4 that the T3 Financial Crime Unit, which TRM formed with Tron and Tether in September 2024, disbanded in late August, with sources citing slow responses and poor coordination. Tether said it would continue working with TRM through Beacon.

Although Redbord called for greater coordination, he warned that such efforts may do little to deter future attacks. He noted that North Korea’s Oct. 29 theft was equivalent to roughly a third of the country’s GDP and accounted for half of the $12.5 billion stolen by state-backed hackers since 2017. Even if attackers recover only $1 billion, the potential payoff remains enormous, he argued.

“North Korea will keep doing this as long as it’s effective,” he said.

Redbord argued that stopping such attacks requires offensive action from governments in response to North Korea’s use of hackers operating as an arm of the state.

TRM’s capacity gap report found that the gap between attackers and cybersecurity defenders continues to widen, driven by hackers’ early adoption of frontier technologies such as AI. He said law enforcement and compliance teams need AI to find and patch vulnerabilities as quickly as hackers can exploit them.

“Every time North Korea hacks, we should be hacking them back,” he continued, calling for offensive cyber operations and action against laundering networks and banks that hackers use to cash out. “This is cyber war,” he said.

#4984. What Running the New York City Marathon Topless After Breast Cancer Was Actually Like

Following her historic NYC Marathon run, Lauren Spiezia opens up to Women’s Health in an exclusive essay about the thing that surprised her the most.

On Sunday, I ran the 26.2 miles of the New York City Marathon completely topless to raise awareness for breast cancer and show people what survival really looks like. Despite all the training, the experience still surprised me.

My legs hurt. My quads cramped at mile 16. The wind chapped my chest. I thought about my form. I thought of my husband Elio, who was running beside me, and whether he was okay. I thought about how grateful I was to run with him and to have a family and support system waiting for me at the finish line and throughout the course. I thought about what a privilege it is to wake up and move my body. And I thought, repeatedly, about not stopping.

Before my run, I wrote that I wanted to do this so I could inspire other women to advocate for themselves and feel okay in their bodies. I ran with a QR code temporarily tattooed on my back that led to a free Tyrer-Cuzick breast cancer risk assessment in hopes that the attention could highlight one of the tools to help women understand their risk. When I finally crossed the finish line, my heart was so full. We had raised over $50,000 for cancer research, helping the very hospital where I had received my own treatment just one year prior.

What I was not thinking about was the fact that I was topless. That was the strangest part. Some people have wondered how I ran without a sports bra. The answer is that because my surgeries had severed the nerves in my chest, I didn’t feel anything. By the time I was at the starting line with the gun minutes away, that decision had already been made a year ago. All that was left was to run.

Mid-race, I passed a group of girls holding a sign that read “This is Women in Sports.” I locked eyes with them. They pointed at me, jumping up and down. Their sign was a nod to a recent sports betting ad that had people talking about women, sexuality, and the representation of female athletes. These girls were making their own statement. This—the marathon and its record 28,942 women running it—was women in sports. I almost backtracked against the current of runners to hug them, but the race carried me on.

There were, of course, plenty of jaws that dropped. That was expected. But I didn't anticipate how many people would cheer me on—from the women on the sidelines who put their hands on their chests in solidarity when I passed them, to fellow runners encouraging me as we ran side-by-side even though I could barely hear them over the crowd, to the volunteers who handed out water cups, telling me they had the same scars.

That was the biggest surprise. It wasn’t the hecklers or the attention, but the love from strangers on the best day in New York City. The response since has been overwhelming.

My inbox is full of women and men I’ve never met. Some are people in treatment. Some just finished treatment. Some whose mothers, daughters, sisters, and aunts were just diagnosed. Women I have never met who want to ask me whether they can see my reconstruction so they can make the decision for themselves. The answer is always yes.

I have also received the opposite. Comments calling me inappropriate. Comments about my size, my choice to reconstruct, my decision to use implants. Comments that I was doing this for attention or for the camera.

Most of the shaming has come after, not during. On the course, people either cheered or stared. But online, people typed.

I made a conscious decision to read them. Not because they do not sting. They do. But because I know that the women I most want to reach—the ones sitting in a chemo chair, the ones who just got a biopsy result, the ones bracing to look at their chests in their bathroom mirror for the first time—are also reading the comments on my page right now. If they see me stay quiet or disappear, they will learn that is what women with bodies like ours should do.

To the people shaming me, I would say this. When you shame a woman’s body online or in person, you do more than silence her. You keep us from talking clinically about our bodies. You keep us from making informed decisions. You keep us from going to the doctor. You keep us from advocating for ourselves. You keep us quiet.

Breast cancer does not care about your opinion on my implants. It does not care whether you think my reconstruction counts. Breast cancer comes for 1 in 8 U.S. women in their lifetimes, and can affect men too. If breast cancer does one day impact someone you love, you will have wanted the women who went through this before them to have spoken loudly.

In 2024, Louise Butcher ran the London Marathon topless with her own mastectomy scars uncovered. She paved the way for women like me. After I first had the idea of running the New York City Marathon topless, I reached out to her to ask about her experience. We spoke again after marathon day. She checked in on the race and on my mental health. She understood, in a way almost no one else can, what the commentary around this kind of run can do to a person. Her message to me was simple. There will always be noise. There will always be criticism. Remember your why.

Louise and I also both noted how outlets have either blurred or strategically covered my chest. A mastectomy removes the breast tissue, and in my case, it removed the nipples too. Nothing on my chest needs censoring or blurring. There is just my chest—a body with prosthetics. Any blurring suggests there is something explicit worth hiding, when there isn’t.

There is also a thread of criticism that suggests some reconstruction choices are more “legitimate” than others. Louise chose flat closure. I chose implants. Neither of us is right or wrong. These are deeply personal choices, and every woman deserves the space to make hers without being audited for authenticity.

I understand the marathon is a family event and that not everyone will agree with the choice I made. Cancer happens to families too. But I hope my run started some important conversations.

Every photo and video I have seen of myself from race day shows the same thing. A young woman who built herself back up, brick by brick. I’m smiling and full of joy. The light that cancer had dimmed in me is now back.

If my run reminded even one woman to go for her annual check, to investigate a suspicious lump, to advocate for herself when a doctor dismisses her, the run was worth it. The scrutiny does not change that.
